BOWLING ASSOCIATION. MR. A. H. CROSFIELD DISTRIBUTES

PRIZES.

IMPORTANT AMALGAMATION PROPOSAL.

The victory of Rylands's team in the,club champion- ship competition of the Warrington and District Bowling Association made it appropriate that the annual concert and prize distiibution should be held on the premises of the recreation club of Messrs. Rylands Bros. Ltd. There was a large muster of bowling enthusiasts in the splendidly-appointed 11 pavilion on Saturday evening and a cordial welcome was extended to Mr. A. H. Crosfield who, as president of the association, attended to band the cup, medals and prizes to the victors. Amongst others present were: Messrs W Peter Rylands, C E Parker, G Ball, jun. (chairman of the Bowlicg Association), J ood (secretary),J Willett (chaitman of the Works Bowling Association), W Patrick (secretary), J Critchley (eecre- tary of the bowling section of Bylands' Recreation Club), Knott and Reddish. Apologies for non- attendance were received from theiMayor (Councillor Dr Joseph), Sir Gilbert Greenall Bart •, Mr Harold Smith, M.P., Captain H Crosfield; Mr Roger C Parr, Mr II Elope and Mr W Carson. An excellent musical programme had been arranged and evoked much appreciation. Messrs J W Willett mid T Humphreys acquitted themselves well in solos and were also associated in the always popular duets "Watchman, what of the night ? " and "The battle eve" which' were most effectively given. Mr Alfred Grounds , created great merriment with his humorous sketches and also introduced a blending of philosophy and comedy in a monologue about shoes. Mr. Bert Smith was an efficient accompani,t. Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD, in submitting the health of "Toe King," said that in these days those who were , called upon to assume responsibility at the bead of the nation's affairs were a great deal less to te envied than the majority of people were apt to think. The burden , imposed upon them seemed to increase with the passage of time, but we bad already had evidence that the present King was in no mind to shirk the responsibili- ties so ably discharged by his predecessors. (Applause). In presenting the Crosfield cup to Mr T Humphreys, captain of Rylands' team, Mr CROSFIELD offered his hearty congratulations to the victors. He did not think there was any reason to be ashamed in looking back on the record of old Warrington in many different I branches of sport,and he was particularly glad that that recreation club, inaugurated so largely by his friend Mr W P Rylands, was so much to the fore and was giving unmistakable evidence that it meant to achieve great things and to assist in maintaining the reputation of Warrington in the howling world. (Applause.) Mr I:Melina:Ye accepted the trophy and said he was very proud that. Rylands' were at the top of the tree this season. If they would only stick together, they would not only be able to retain the cup but would also have a strong chance of winning the Lancashire county cup. (Applause.) Itir CROSFIELD nest presented a clock and case of cutlery, to Mr Harrison, captain of the Cemetey Hotel team, who were the runners-up. He said it was not the first time he had had to congratulate his old friends t f the Cemetery Hotel on the winning of prizes and their continued success was a matter of great personal satis- faction to him. (Applause.) Mr HARRISON, in returning thanks, congratulated Bylands' on their victory. It ass a new club and deserved great credit for having defeated the two senior teams in Warrington. Outside organizations had been a littlo bit afraid of entering the competition: they had been so afraid of Cemetery and Bull's Head that they thought nobody else would have a chanae. (Laughter.) Rylands' had,however, showed What it was possible for young and persevering teams to do, and be roped that next seeson there would he a larger entry. ' (Applause). The consolation competition was won for the second time by the Liberal Club and, in receiving the award, Mr E Walker expressed the hope that they would in the near future improve on past achievements by winning 1 the senior tourney. Penketh Tannery were the runners-up. Mr GEORGE BALL, jun., was the winner of the individual merit competition. Mr Crosfield jocularly remarked that he had met acme" pot hunters" ia the golfing wrorld, but he did not remember any golfer at the moment, who could quite hold a candle to Mr Ball in the capacity of prize winner. (Laughter and 1 applause.) Mr BALL, who received a handsome watch and chain, said they bad had a very good competition and his sympathy went out to the losers. No-one enjoyed a good fight at bowls more than he did, and there had not been a keener competition in the history of the association. He was very fortunate in receiving the prize, for there were two games in which he had got home by a single point. He had never played better opponents in his lite ; the bowling was keen and clean. , (Applause). Mr 3 W JOLLEY received the second prize and said Bull's Head meant to capture the cup from Itylauds' next year. Mr RYLANDS then proposed the health of the Presi- dent of the Association (Mr AII Crosfield) and, in the "name of the club, offered him a hearty welcome. Air Crosfield was an old Warringtonian and a thorough sportsman. He did not think the anociation could , have a more typical Warrington sportsman than Mr Crosfield as its president. He was with them when they ' opened that recreation ground and pavilion, and he had taken a very keen interest in the welfare of the club.' (Applause). The toast was honoured with great enthusiasm, the company singing" For he's a jolly good fellow." Mr. CROsFIELD, in respondingt recalled his attend- ,' ance at the opening of the receatlon ground, ana said I Mr. Rylands was right in saying that he had felt a whole-hearted sympathy and interest in its development and success. It was not necessary for any of them to advocate and support games as a mere end in themselves, but in these days of increasing competition and strain, the importance of old English pastimes in all our indus- trial centres, was undeniable. It amounted to more than mere recreation—it was, in a very real sense, a matter of necessity and he for one was exceedingly glad to notice that our friends on the Continent were I taking a leaf out of our hot-I.c in that respect(Applause.) In some respects—such as town planning, education and chemical research—we could profit from the example of Continental nations, but on the other hand he was glad to know that there was not a little that they could learn from old England. (Applause.) They were now not only realizing the enormous advantage of field sports in their effect on physique and oharacter, but were beginning to appreciate the desirability of adequate weekly leisure in which to enjoy these healthy recreations. While he was in Parliament and since his good friend Mr Rylands and a few others bad given him a position of greater freedom and less responsibility—(laughter)—he had taken an active interest in the work of the International Asso- ciation for Labour Legislation. This organization, which had already proved of great practical utility aimed at comparing notes between different countries discovering where improved conditions and better hours of labour were practicable without increased cost of production, and taking steps, where that kind of improvement did mean heavier cost, to put all produccrs on a level footing—(hear, bear)—by intro- ducing the reform simultaneously in the different countries by international convention. Now there was a strong movement iu favour of an eight hours shift instead of twelve hours, in all industries that worked night and day in Europe and the United States. On those lines gratifying progress was being made on the Continent and, in conjunction with it, was the effort to establish the weekly half-holiday which was already customary in England. It was natural that, along with that movement, they were awakening to the advantage of games as a profitable way of fillip" part of their holidays. That was all to the good, for better physique meant not only better working capacity but a fuller appreciation of the intellectual advantages which modern civilization offered to those who were able to enjoy them. (Hear, hear). That was a work in which we could make common cause with our friends on the ld.annmdd(Aeaepwvtpaaal sras uet shtgeyao. t)oo good Tv other trrthiyheeenr dvbssi pog owionrrl toi itnnubgage, Continent. That was a tradition well worthy to be maintained. (Hear, hear.) Finally, it gave him additional pleasure to attend that gathering because be was on the premises started in connection with a great works which was leading the way in what might aptly be called " the civilizing of industry." Over their home course Hallarus.hire placed J Cook 1, L Chatta%Nay 2, J E Deakin (of Herne Hill and Olympic tame) 3,D Aldows (brother of the international) 4, W Chattaway 5 W Butcher 6, F Smith 8, E Leadbetter 9, A Oldfield 12 ; whilst Rotherham were counting J Holden 7, D Hanson 10, and A Smith 11. II Daley is reputed to have covered a three miles course in 15 mins. 45 sec. in beating T Chilton, D Hur.t and others, so Bolton must have a use- ful novice if the distance is correct.

Warrington A.C. iaa two packs over a 51 miles course. OLBOY SWIMMING CHAM

'IONS.

WILLIAM O'NEIL (Centre), WINNER OF THE SCHOOLBOY CHAMPIONSHIP OF WARRINGTON. Names of the Squadron Team: WILFRED BRADY, JOHN 11ILLINGTON, JOSEPH NOLAN and THOMAS MULLANEY:

On Tuesday last the 'Thornton Challenge Bowl " for the school boy championship of Warrington and the "Walker Challenge Shield " for the best school boy squadron team were formally presented to the boys of St Mary's Schools Buttermarket by Cllonel Reid Walker who was accompanied by Mr J Fulton Smith and Mr A Barlow. After the boys had assembled and sang one of their school songs Colonel Reid Walker con- gratulated the boys on winning both trophies for their school. He then gave a very interesting little address during the course of which he reviewed the great swirnming feats of the past and advised the boys to continue to improve themselves in the art of swimming so that if ever the occasion arose it would be of benefit to themselves and others. He expressed the hope that some day they might receive the highest reward that a civilian could obtain, viz., the medal of the Royal Humane Society. The Rev N Wilson, 0.5.13., who presided, thanked Colonel Walker for his kindness in

being present and spoke of the value of swimming in develeping I be qualities of pluck,andurance and presence of mind. Mr J Fulton Smith spoke of the value of never being flustered and being ever ready even at a moment's notice to say or do whatever may be required. —Mr A Barlow, superintendent of the Baths, was con• gratulated on the efficiency of his instruction in the art of swimming.—Three hearty cheers were given by the boys in recognition of the kindness of Colonel Reid Walker, and then all joined in the singing of the Nivional Anthem.

During the course of the proceedings the winners were preFented with medals and the following boys received certificates for proficiency in swimming : William O'Neil, John Millington, Thomas Graham, Charles Graham, Wilfrid Brady. Joseph Nolan, Thomas Mullaney, George Watson., William Miller, Alfred Jewitt, Victor Vernon and James Jones.

===== 0002601_19121109_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
Prices began to rise all over the world in 1895 or 1896. The extent of the rise in Great Britain, Germany and the United States is shown in our diagram which is based on figures given by Mr. R. H. Hooker to the Royal Statistical Society last year. The rise has been greatest in Germany, and least in the United Kingdom. To make this diagram clear it should he explained that -Mr. Hooker took as his base the average prices in each country between 1890 and 1899, called this 100 and constructed his diagram from this base. Hence it affords no ground for comparison of actual prices in the different countries, but only shows the rise from above average in each country. We see that in the United States prices rose 28 per cent, in Germany 42 per cent, while in Free Trade Britain, only 14 per cent. This is not surprising, for th country which adopts a policy (we call it Free Trade) which enables it in time of scarcity or failure of crops in one locality freely to draw its supplies from any land in which there is plenty, naturally keeps any advance down to the natural advance due to a worldwide scarcity. On the other hand, those countries forced to rely on their own supplies through the shutting out of the world's bounty, naturally suffer from local scarcity due to climatic causes aggravated by economic foolishness.

The widow claimed £3OO, but only .£192 was awarded. Liverpool are more level headed than those of Lendon."

THE QUEST OF TRUTH. CANON STEVENSON CRITICISES MODERN

JOURNALISM.

The Rev. Canon MORLEY STEVENSON gave an interest- ing address before the members of ihe Warrington Philomathic Society at the Old Academy on WedimsdaY evening. He referred to the main object of the societY which was to search after truth. The greater number of people, he said, did not search after the truth but only after that which.supported a particular set Of opinions or the party to which they belonged. It woula be a valuable asset to the community if they could get a body of people who would seek after truth for the sake of truth and not for the sake of scoring off some- body else. Most English people were the readers of only one daily newspaper, " invariably read two newspapers of totally different complexions every day," commented the Canon. "I have my Radical newspaper at breakfast and my Con- servative paper when I have time for it. I find in that very great safety, because I don't get one side of the question only, but I get both sides put before me in a very skilful manner. it seems a rather horrid thing to say, and I don't mean it in a nasty spirit, but the writers of newspapers do not wish to write the truth. What they want to do is to put the attitude of their own party and the opinions of their own people in the most favourable and convincing light possible. Until someone is bold enough to publish a daily‘paper which will be absolutely and fearlessly true, regardless of any party but simply desiring to get at the very heart of things, we must content ourselves with reading tw?, papers and drawing a middle course between them. The Canon further contended that the ethics of Journalism were off the straight line'; it must inevitably be so. Newspapers did not tell us of the things that generally happened, but of events which were unusual or startling. We must not therefore expect to get a real, true view of things from our daily newspaper. The search after truth was not only difficult in itself, but it led one into extraordinarilY difficult situations. Truth did not appear the same to all people because they did not all view it from the same standpoint. Although in or e sense they cculd never agree about truth, in another they might all see' what was true, because it presented itself in different aspects to different individuals. The search after truth might make them extremely unpopular and they might even have to break ties which where very strong One of the most difficult situations a man could find himself in would be that he was convinced that a certain thing was true and yet if he followed his con- victions it would mean the severing of associations and almost the breaking of his heart in doing so. That was the sacrifice which truth expected from those who would follow her but if a man made the sacrifice be bought a place of mind which he could not get in SOY other way. The love of truth and wisdom would not only mike them searchers after truth but would give them that culture and gentleness which would soften manners, increase courtesy, and strengthen their lei" ence aherever they went. They would not only do a great service in standing for what was true, hut would so upraise the culture of their nature that they would sweeten society and introduce to it that which would: pass through it like leaven and raise, uplift and elevate , it in every possible way. (Applause.) The Rev Dr STANLEY MELLOR, who opened the dis- cussion and moved a vote of thanks to Canon Stevenson said the Canon was perfectly right in his view that the journalistic tone of this country was excessivell Partisan—partisan to the extent not only of keeping the' truth back but actually perverting and destroying it.lt was a sign of the extraordinaty condition into whicb the whole mind of civilization had permitted itself to , drift—a condition in which there seemed to be not only a neglect of truth in the deep an'., wide sense, but a total disregard of it. Vie,'" ing modern civilization from the outside, o 0? would suppose that the only truths that society cared about were illusions. We seemed to live upon illusions and to keep ourselves going by them. It was vers: seldom that the voice of truth was really heard, son the man who spoke up generally suffered for it. One of the most hopeful signs howerer, was that even In this world of illusions, there was growing up a distinct desire for the truth. They found it in all classes ot society, and as that demand spread it would alter the whole complexion of society. Many theological snd political questions were not matters of absorute truth; but simply of opinion. If they raised the question 01- what kind of social organization was to replace tbe present decaying one, it was a question on which therse. was -room ior differences of opinion. In such field! they had to learn to be tolerant of those who did a°' see eye to eye with them. Mr T E KNOWLES seconded and amongst other6i taking part in the debate were Messrs N Neild, HedleY Taylor, E 0 Burgess, E Walsh, and the President (slr James Ball). • The PRESIDENT put in a word for mddern journalisln' contending that it was only in the region of politics that newspapers were biased and that passing events were quite accurately recorded. Canon STEVENSON, in reply, said it was a sign °1.14, healthy mental condition when they changed their views on certain subjects. He should rather dread trn't day when he began to find that his views did 10 change at all. He should believe that he was fossilis- ing and that his power for gaining and increasing In the knowledge of truth was leaving him.

NOTES BY " MUST."

Warrington are no sooner in than out of the Lanca- shire Cup Competition. They had opportunities at St Helens of making further progress, but the oppor- tunities were wasted and the Wirepullers deservedly beaten. The score of IS points to ten flattered the Saints, for the result of the match was really in doubt until the last five minutes, and a return of Warring- ton's old cup-tie form might have reversed the verdict.

I never saw Warrington shape more indifferently at winning a cup tie. They began badly, recovered themselves in a way worthy of admiration, and then frittered away the golden opportunity of victory by bad tactics. As a rule, the Wirepullers are good tactician in cup-tie warfare, but on Saturday they were seriously at fault and badly led. The game opened with Warrington strongly aggressive and St Llelens were lucky to escape without conceding any Points after Turtill had twice had a narrow escape on his own line. Then came the first and most disastrous blunder. A lofty punt from one of the home backs was sent into the "Warrington quarters. The ball should have been left to Tranter or Dickenson but Rushton rushed in, missed the catch, and gave the home forwards a soft try under the posts, which was easily converted. This was about the most notable thing Rushton did, but it seems to have earned him his place en the team.

One could sympathise with the team as a whole under this misfortune, and they set about putting matters to rights in deadly earnest. Brooks was very aggressive, and eventually he robbed Barton in his own quarter and scored a dashing try behind the posts which Jolley converted. Woodburn had a chance to send Tranter in easily just before the interval, but he delayed his return pass too long and was collared by the full back. The teams thus turned round on equal terms, but Warrington had had much the better of the game in this half.

On restarting, the first five minutes revealed the astonishing fact that Warrington were playing for a draw—about the silliest possible policy with 40 minutes to go. They made marks; they let the ball go dead; and when it was brought out again for the drop-out Thomas wasted as much time as he could. All this silly travesty of football could have only one result. St. nelens were flattered by Warrington's want of enter- prise and with an established position in the Warrington half they began to open out the game. Several openings went astray, but points were bound to come, and after one of the halves had dropped a goal, Barton scored a couple of tries at the corner in a very short time. Then Warrington wakened up, only to find that 30 minutes had gone, and they were somewhat lucky to wipe 5 points out as the result of Woodburn following up a long kick which beat the St. Helens backs. St. Helens scored again and the match was over. I suppose it is a waste of time telling experienced footballers that the best defence is attack but Saturday was the first occasion on which I have seen any team set about playing for a draw with half the game in front of them. Such tactics deserved to fail. They were not only silly, but an insult to the spectators.

There is not a great deal to be said about individual performers. The ‘tiarrington forwards scrummaged well and followed up speedily, but they were not so Clever at handling the ball as the home six. The latter did some smart passing, and both Barton's tries .came from openings made by forwards. St. Helens have long modelled their loose play on Colonial lines, and 'Warrington's speedy forwards would do well to Copy the example. There is too much being tackled With the ball on the Warrington side. If a man is in bis opponent's half he should see that a fellow player gets the ball before the tackler, comes along. In other words combination is the secret of success, and Warrington's combination is a long way below par.

Our half backs were very poor on Saturday. Rushton was feeble ia the extreme and the playing of Douglas as scrum half placed this promising junior at a dis- advantage. He tackled splendidly, however, which is more than could be said for his partner. Tranter and Brooks were the hest of the threequarters, and the fact that they are not playing on the same Wing is a mistake. Brooks would ecore more tries on the right with Tranter as a partner than he does - alongside Dickenson. The latter was slower than usual on Saturday, and did not field the ball at all accurately. In the extreme rear -Jolley was never safer than Turtill, though he has not the cleverness of the New Zealander at turning defence int.° attack. The youngster might run more when he has the ball, and indulge in some of the tricky foot- work which was a feature of his earlier trials.

_ LANCASHIRE JUNIOR CUP.—Second Round. Cadishead lO Flixton Rylands 6 Runcorn W. S 5 Swinton 5 Hollinswood R 3 Swinton P Wardley Irlam Parish Church a bye. WIDNES OUTCLASSED AT SALFORD. Salford played excellent football at Weaste in the Lancashire Cup tie, and thoroughly deserved the victory they registered against Widnes. There were 6,000 people present, and only five minuteS.had elapsed before Healey got possession, and dashed over at the corner the same player failing at goal. Evan Thomas also scored, Mesley goaling, while close to the interval Lanoce dropped a magnificent goal. In the second half the Widnes backs showed no improvement, and were invariably defending. Noon and Swinton allowed the Salford right wing to get a clear opening, and Loveluck passing to W. Thomas, the latter scored. Wild was also credited with a try, Mesley failing at gcral on each occasion. Towards the finish Laws registered an 1 unimproved try for the visitors. Result :—Salford, 2 goals, 4 tries (16 points) ; Widnes, 1 try (3 points.) .Teams and officials :

Salford—Launce, back ; Wild, W Thomas, Loveluck, and Mesley, three-quarter backs ; May and John, half backs; 1 J Thomas, Rees, Ritchie, Thom, Bevan, and Currie, forwards. Widnes—Jones, back ; Swinton, Pennington, Lally and Laws three-quarter backs ; Noon and O'Garra, half backs; G Aspey, S Aspey, Cosgrove, Lloyd, H O'Garra, and Reid, forwards. Referee, G F Dickenson, Halifax. Touch judges, H Ogden, Oldham, and A Johnson, Rochdale.

The Examiner, Saturday, November 9, 1912_

Runcorn were only able to put a weak team in the fb4d on Wednesday for this re-arranged league match. Salford were short of Wild and Launce, Harrison re-oppearing after a long absence duo to injuries. At the outset Salford playea with great vigour and a fine bout of passing between Lovelock, John and Mesley ended in the wing man leaping over an opponent and scoring. Thomas added the goal. Runcorn were repeatedly dangerous, and a grand dribble by Thornton deserved a better end. Obstruction against Inrann gave Mesley the chance of kicking a penalty goal, and Runcorn were thus seven poiuts in arrears at the interval.

The second half witnessed great improvement in Runcorn, and.they pressed without being able to score. The hall was flung about freely by both sides, but Salford showed the better finish. A sudden breakaway by John and May ended in Lovelock giving to Mesley who registered a great try after running from the centre, but the same player failed at goal. Penalties frequently accrued to both sides, chiefly for obstruction but both Mesley and Richardson failed to kick goals. Meslev was successful from a mark later, and just on time Runcorn's determination was rewarded. One of the Salford backs mulled, and Hughes dashing up scored between the posts, Inman kicking the goal. Result : Salford, 3 2 12; Runcorn, 1 1 5. HUDDERSFIELD'S BIG SCORE.

Bramley and Huddersfield met on Wednesday in a re-arranged League fixture at Bramley. Result :—Huddersfield, 9 11 51; Bramley, 3 3 15.

LANCASBIRE JUNIOR CUP. SECOND ROUND.

FLYL ANDS' RECREATION v. RUNCORN WHITE STAR.

Interest in this match, which twas played on the Gorsey-lane enclosure on Saturday, rauiexceptionally high and there was an excellent attendance. Play throughout was very keen. The home side suffered a loss in the first few minutes, for Horrocks was so badly hurt that he had to retire. The home goat was subjected to severe pressure, but &timbal', Leah and Hennessey were smart on the defence. Capital forward work gave Jolley serious trouble and during a hot raid on the Rtancorn goal two players were ordered off. Eventually the visitors were penalized and from a long range Bramhall gave the home team the lead. Both lines had narrow ()shapes after this, but the defence prevailed and at the interval the home side led by two points. In the second stage Rylands' soon augmented their lead with another penalty goal. A round of passing led up to a try for Runcorn, scored by D Jolley, his brother the custodian adding a goal. In the closing stages Hennessey dropped at goal, and when the referee signalized `that the ball had passed between the posts, there was great jubila- tion amongst the Warrington supporters. Result : Rylands' Recreation three goals (six points) ; Runcorn White Star, one goal one try (live points). THIRD ROUND. Warrington at St. Helens. Bad Tactics by the Wirepullers.

Two well-matched teams were in opposition at Knowsley-road, St Helens, on Saturday where Warring- ton were the visiting team in the second round for the Lancashire Cup, and the result was in doubt to the end. St Helens put a strong side in the field, and Warrington turned out as selected though some of the Welsh platers were absent through ineligibility, and Renwick (the captain) was unable to play through an injury. Fine weather favoured the fixtures and a crowd of 7,000 con- tributed £l5O in gate-money, which is divided between the contesting clubs. The teams and officials were : St. Helens.—Turtill, back; Flanagan, Greenwood Browning and Barton, three-quarter backs ; Molyneaux and Richardson,half-backs ; Beaten, Shallcross,Mavitty, Waddell, Prescott, and Manchester, forwards. Warrington.—Jolley, back ; Brookes, Dickenson, Tranter, end Woodburn, three-quarter backs; Rushton • and Douglas, half-backs ; Thomas, Skelhorne, Fearnley, Cheaters, Bevan, and Naylor, forwards. Referee, H Edmondson, Bradford. Touch judges, F Ratcliffe, Sainton, and T Roscoe, Warrington. Warrington started with the wind in their favour, and were soon on the attack. Some fine punting by Dickenson, and speedy following up by Cheaters and Skelhorne,resulted in Turtill being chased over his own' goal line, and a touch-down was the result. Lively play in the home quarter followed, and Douglas made a good attempt to break through. Turtill eventually cleared with a long punt which wits completely misjudged by Rushton, and the home forwards dribbled through the Warriugton ranks. Dickeuson and Jolley both tried to save the situation but Manchester snapped up the ball and raeed over under the posts five minutes from the start, Turtill easily adding a goal. This early reverse was Warrington's own fault—or rather Rushton's. The visitors set about altering the position in determined , fashion, and the forwards were very energetic. Brooks made a dashing run, over half the length of the field, but his pass inside to Rushton was mulled, and a good chance came to nought. St. Helens responded with some lively handling, and forced IJolley to clear, the young full back making a fine punt into the St. Helens half. Bevan, Naylor and Thomas rushed the ball to the line and Molyneux was lucky to save close in. On the other wing, Woodburn got going, but numbers told a tale. Then Douglas broke away smartly, and gave to Dickenson, but the veteran missed the pass with a clear field. Warrington were having much the brter of the game and a long punt caused Barton to fl Id on the line. He ran up to the quarter line but . delayed his punt so long that Brooks charged the ball down, and securing smartly, beat Turtill with ease in a powerful run round the posts. Jolley kicked a goal and made the scores equal. 'Molyneux, Green- wood and Flanagan made a raid on the other side, the wing man taking a low pass splendidly only to be pulled down from behind by Woodburn. The Warrington halves next got the bell away at centre, and Tranter fed Woodburn. The Ulverston man ran Inely, but muffed the return pass to Tranter when there was only Turtill to beat—another opening wasted. The interval arrived a moment later with the score live points each. St. Helens were soon aggressive on resuming and ^ Jolley was laid out in making a plucky save from Sballcross. Then Warrington began to indulge in faulty tactics. They apparently theught they could make a drawn game by letting the ball go dead and "marking," regardless of the fact that there was forty minutes play in front of them. The result was that St. Helens established a footing in the Warrington half and were continually on the attack. Richardson and Flanagan both had shots at the Warrington goal without success. Danger was temporarily averted when Tranter pulled up Mavitty when the forward looked likely to go through, but success attended Greenwood, who had is shot at goal from a by no means difficult angle. and the St Helens men once more took the lead. Following on this success, the Warringtonians played with more vigour, and after George Thomas and Bevan had made a little sprint the Warrington defence crumbled before Barton, who, accepting a pass from Prescott, raced in for a try at the corner. St Helens continued to play a rare bustling game and success was made certain when Barton with another capital offset, scored his second try. Warrington made a spasmodic burst and Skelhorne led the way to the half-way line after St Helens had threatened danger. A long kick by Tranter was followed up by Woodburn who took the ball on the bounce and scored trader the bar, for Jolley to add the goal. In the closing stages St Helens were always dangerous, and after Molyneux and Browning bad shown up, Richardson crossed for a try and Molyneux goaled with a huge kick. Result: St Helens, three goals, four tries (18 points) ; Warrington, two goals, two tries (1.0 points). RUNCORN STILL ERRATIC. Runcorn entertained Leigh at Irwell-lane on Satur- day. After Richardson had missed with a couple of easy penalty kicks, Inman tried bis luck and pot the home team ahead. Then the visitors responded, and Cain and Roberts Put in snippy run, the latter scoring,

This postponed tie was decided on the ground of the former. Lymm kicked off and opened their account from a free kick in the first few minutes. A hard struggle followed, both defences being kept fully employed. W Thomasson missed narrowly for the Park, whilst at the other end Simpson cleared finely from Barber. Give-and-take play followed for some time. Ten minutes from the interval Owen (Lymni) ran through and put his side further ahead. Half- time : Lymm, two goals ; Ongbtrington Park, nil. Restarting, the visitors pressed, Hadden, in goal, having to be on the alert to keep his charge intact. At the end of 20 minutes the Park were justly rewarded; following upon a clearance by Bidder), W Thomasson obtained possession and with a good shot reduced the lead. Play now became very keen. Lymm took up the running and when Owen was well placed Lord brought him down unfairly in the dreaded area. Hines took the kick and although Simpson saved his first effort he could not prevent him scoring from the rebound. Up to the finish the Park fought desperately hut the Lymm defence prevailed. Score, Lymm three goals ; Oughtrington Park one goal.

WHIST DRIVES AND TEIE LAW.

The Chief Conitable of St. Helens (Mr. A. R. Ellerington) states that the St. Helens police have carried out the law on the question of whist drives, and so far as he is aware no offence has so far been com- mitted in the district. • The position as understood locally by whist drive promoters is that they must not be run for personal profit or gain, but when carried out in the interests of charity or some local philanthropic object and the prizes are given by people interested in the efforts or charity, then no offence is committed against the law. -

IRISH TEACHERS' GRIEVANCES. To a deputation from the Irish Teachers' Association, which waited on him at the House of Commons, Mr Binell announced that he had decided to appoint s Commibsion of Inquiry into the working of the Board of National Education in Ireland, with a view to , remedying the teachers' grievances.

HOCKEY.

THE COUNTY TRIAL.

In the County Trial beat Cheshire 3-1. The game was contested at a terrific pace. Dobbs (2) Edge (1) scored for Lancashire and Hunt for Cheshire. Cheshire had more of the play than Lanca- shire, but their forwards could make little impression in the circle chiefly OD account of a -vigilance of Hodgson, the St. Anne's full back, who played a wonderful game. Knight was about the best of the Cheshire halves. Partridge at left back played soundly, and was not responsible for any of the goals scored against his side. The ploy of the Cheshire forward line was spoiled through the selfishness of Stewart (Oxton) at inside right, Bainbridge and Travis being the chief sufferers. Stewart repeatedly lost possas;on through not parting with the bell when his %ring man end centre were better placed. Hunt was the pick of the forwards.

LATCHFORD.

The First Eleven Kirby five second team men with them but the latter acquitted themselves all right and a close game ended in a 1-1 draw. West Kirby were leading at the interval but Pearson equalized. Walker at left back for the home team was in free form. Gibbs and Eccles were the best of the visiting backs. The Second Eleven had a very weak side cut at home and West Kirby won 4-2. The visitors were the best team and played good hockey, their forward line being fast and capable, and their full backs were safe and hit hard. The Latchford forwards had an unusually smart goalkeeper to face who cleared splendidly several times. Bradshaw, Panks, and Mull worked bard in the home defence. The First Eleven v Kersal this afternoon is:— Wright, goal • Fletcher, Billiugton, hacks ; Isherwood, Wilkinson, knight, halves ; Dale, Pearson. Travis, Banks, Starkey, forwards. Referee, Mr Cohn. ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S ASSOCIATION. N. A. CENTRE.

There was a great imp' ovemunt in the weather during last week-end but we cannot report a great improve- ment in fish. Those who visited the Gowy had practically a blank day and after the recent rains there should have been better results, but the visitors declare that it was practically unfishable owing to the rush of water and the deep colour. However the fish should come on feed when it fines down. A few sharp frosts seem to put the Gowy fish on feed better than any- thing and the best results are taken after a spell of frosty weather. _ THE PRIZE LIST.

We stated last v-ia-s-d-c-tilr-ti-t-ii; prize list would be in print by this week, but it was owing to an oversight in looking through the minute book that it appeared the set board was closed. We find that it is the last day in November and not the first day so that gives members one or two more outings before we close. FISHING IN THE NORTH. OVEReOATS.

There should be general ENGLAND AND satisfaction with the THE WAR. statements made by Sir

Edward Grey in the House of Commons as to our relation with the situation in Eastern Europe. He said, in reply to searching question's that no one, which means none of the Powers—will be disposed to dispute the rights of the Balkan States to formulate the terms on which they will be disposed to conclude peace. He does not think the Great Powers have bsen or will be more slow than any other people, "to adjust their own views to the march of events." That means a complete accLptance of the situation created by the tremendous and crushing victories over the Turks. What- ever may now take place, we may feel pride and confidence in the attitude assumed by our own Government. France and Great Britain may fairly be regarded as the two disinterested Powers. France is in possession of all that she has been longing for. Great Britain is not looking for any benefits from the dissolution of the Ottoman Empire. Great Britain and France may be expected to work together in a spirit of unselfishness in behalf of a solution of the Eastern question that will change its character, so that it shall be no longer a perpetual threat and a festering sore. The Foreign Secretary further used some important and illuminating words in answer to questions as to whether the Porte has signified its readiness to welcome assistance in bringing about the suspension of hostilities. He said that the Powers are exchanging views. It is, and must be, he added, a very delicate matter for these Powers to interfere between belligerents, and they can only do so at the bequest of both. These also are, in Lord Beaconsfield's phrase, the words of sense and truth. If an armistice is to be arranged, it I must be at the request of both belligerents. Nothing must be done, and, in fact, nothing can be done, which would have the effect of breaking the impact of the war on either of the parties to it. The Powers cannot in fair- ness intervene in such a matter as would either prevent an immediate rally of the Turks or stand in the way of the completion of their victories by the Balkan States. On grounds of humanity it might be desirable to stop the war, but there can be no justifica- tion for stopping it under such conditions as must necsssarily react unfavourably either on one side or the other.

There is something rather nauseating in the Turkish representation that the Ottoman Government not only desires peace now, but has desired peace all along, in fact, is de- votedly and continuously in favour of peace. That seems to be thin and foolish hypocrisy; Let us place against it the statement of a more than usually reasonable Turkish news- paper, which says, "We do not wish to speak of the past because it is painful ; but, had we applied the Constitution properly, Europe would not have ranged herself again a us, and the, Balkan States would not have dared to rise against us." Nothing could be more true. But repentance for the past is now much too late, and if 'it is sincere in this particular instance, it is very far indeed from being a general sentiment. Whatever may now take place, the Turks must expect oniy a reasonable consideration, and not a special decision in their favour. They have too long been engaged in doing their worst to havo any title to particular commisseration in their disasters. They will have to be con- sidered merely fa; an element in a large problem, not as a European Power that must be kept in a place to which it has long ceased to be entitled.

Mr. J. Jarman tackles POLITICS IN an old and very thorny MUNICIPAL problem politics in ELECTIONS, municipal elections—in a letter which appears on another page. Most people will agree with him that party political nominees are not always the best men who could be brought forward, and also that very frequently they are the wrong men entirely to entrust with the management of a town's affairs. This is not because they are party men, but because, the party system brings men to the forefront who would otherwise remain in the obscurity which they are more fitted to adorn. We could all point a finger to individual examples. Mr. Jarman makes some sugges- tions worthy of consideration. The idea of a Selection Committee appears to be quite impracticable in a town like Warring- ton where men are so sharply divided politically, and it is open to the further objection that its tendency would be to dis- franchise the ratepayers. With them, after all, should rest the choice of municipal representatives. Frankly, we have no present hope of seeing politics excluded from municipal contests. But many of the objec- tionable features might be removed by the voluntary abandonment of canvassing—or better still by its compulsory abolition—and the restriction of the use of vehicles. Our correspondent makes useful suggestions on these lines, and we should welcome the views of other townsmen who have given some thought to the perplexities and drawbacks of municipal representation.

Mr. S. F. Edge is one of FORTUNES UNDER the men who dined FREE TRADE, with the Duke and handed over £l,OOO for Tariff Reform. Mr. Edge also, is a well- known motor manufacturer who is con- stantly declaring that the industry needs protection. It is clear enough from the amount of trade done and the way the industry is growing. that it needs nothing of the sort ; and Mr. Edge himself is the finest proof of that. He has just retired from active business life, and in an interview in "The Motor" he says that " The business of S F. Edge. Limited, is better than it has ever been ; we shall this year have made about S:60.000, wnereas last year the profits were about 5...36,000. . . . The motor business has been a bridge to fortune for me, and I shall have a tender feeling for it, even if only on that account." Perhaps what the tender-hearted Mr. Edge wants is to " protect " the other motor firms from the competition of a company that has nearly doubled its profits in one year. Any- how, S. F. Edge, Limited, has done pretty well without protection against the foreign- made car, hasn't it?

As might have been DR JEKYLL AND MR. expected, the case of HYDE. the young engineer who has been sentenced I to imprisonment for stealing a motor-car, in spite of the defence of automatism, is evoking considerable public attention and discussion in the correspondence columns of the Press. The case is certainly one of peculiar interest, for the plea of double identity has according to the Judge at Derby Assizes, never previously been raised in the courts. It is, however, a mistake to regard the phenomenon of dual personality as merely an ingenious and fascinating theme for the novelist. Possibly the grim contrast between Jekyll and Hyde may not be so vividly apparent in the realm of actuality—such benevolence and gentility on the one hand and such horrible wickedness c)it the other—but we know that the baffling problem of good and evil alternately domin- ating the action of individuals, without the consciousness of the change manifesting itself unto them, is a rare but an established fact. When, however, the plea of automatism is raised as a defence in our criminal courts, what has hitherto been merely an engrossing subject of scientific and psychic investigation becomes fraught with issues of considerable importance. How far may Jekyll be punished for the villainies of Hyde? Of course, it is not difficult to imagine the plea becoming a favourite among all manner of criminals who are brought face to face with the prospect of punishment for their offences, but we suppose the cases of genuine dualism are few and far between, and that the fraud of the others must be easy of proof, In the case at Derby for instance, a specialist contributing to the discussion on the subject considers the circumstances indicated that the prisoner was in a state of "wandering epilepsy." Dual personality may, of coun-e, in many cases, be reduced to terms of physical disease; the "psychic problem" may have no existence. In such cases the subject is apparently one for doctors rather than lawyers.

So far as it affects this THE NEW AMERICAN country, the American PRESIDENT. election is chiefly regarded from the point of view of commerce. One of the greatest steps towards the easier interchange of goods is being taken in the construction of the Panama Canal. When that vital water-way is open the balance of trade will be disturbed; distant places will become near; inacessible products of nature will become available. The attitude of the new President and his party towards the international problems 'raised by the completion of the Canal is all- important. What is President Wilson's purpose? ' The official programme of the Democratic Party, on which Wilson went to election, declared: "We favour the exemption from tolls of American ships engaged in coastwise trade passing through the Panama Canal." This is a rather non- committal tone, for it leaves open the question whether foreign ships are to pay tolls or not. In his Speech of Acceptance Wilson was more emphatic, though not much more explicit. He said it would be a little ridiculous if, after building this "great international highway," America should have no ships to send through it. The Canal was certainly not "merely for the use of our men-of-war." How were they to increase the number of American merchant vessels, now, of course, pitifully small? Not, said Wilson, "by the mere paltry device of tolls. We must build and buy ships in competition with the world." There should be coupled with this the speech in which Wilson spoke of the present American tariff wall as a "darn," choking and hemming in the trade activities of the most vigorous people on the face of the earth. So far, then, as the new President may find it possible to carry out the policy of his party and his own ideals, we may expect to see American shipping becoming in the course of time a real competitor with British shipping, and the American export trade, which will be assisted by the Canal and the ships, increas- ing still more in volume. The process will be slow, but it will be relentless, and nothing that we can do to prevent it.

While controversy is THE NEW raging over the banning CENSORSHIP. of Professor Reinhardt's latest wordless play, "A Venetian Night," there comes the news that another Censorship has been set up. Mr. G. A. Redford. whose •career as the Examiner of Plays progressed through such stormy waters, has been appointed "Censor of

Films" by the Associated Cinematograph Manufacturers, and the new Censorship will be in operation by the end of the year. We imagine it will excite a good deal less controversy than does the other Censorship, and Mr. Redford's announcement of the lines along which his preliminary activities are to lie is certainly promising. It is proposed to issue two forms of certificates,one for general purposes and one for children's matinees. Special care will be taken by himself and his assistants that films for the latter will be entirely suitable for the youth- , ful mind, and by way of securing the co-operation of the various cine- matograph halls he will invite them to exhibit notices on their walls.guaranteeing that the pictures shown by them have all been approved by the Censor. This is in itself an important move in the right direction. Many of the shows at present are quite unsuitable for the audiences,largely composed of children, who crowd to them and it would be a very good thing if arrangements could be made that children should only be allowed to take seats in the cinema halls when the pro- gramme is one arranged for them. On more general lines Mr. Redford, we see, proposes to discourage Biblical subjects, and to keep a close eye upon burglary pictures and mixed bathing idylls, while there will be little chance for films representing Sovereigns, Judges, Ministers, or other constitutional authorities in ridiculous or indecorous situa- tions. These are all prominent features at the moment, and if Mr. Redford succeeds in sweeping them away, and in doing something to meet the difficulty of children's shows, he will make a very good start. There will be plenty of problems to follow.

The Chief School Medi- THE TEETH OF OUR cal Officer for the SCHOOL CHILDREN. county of Cheshire Dr. Meredith Young, has just brought out his report for the year 1911. From this we quote some interesting remarks on the condition of the teeth of the school children in the elementary schools of Ch-shire.

Dr Young estimates that the teeth of at least one cluldin every twenty show signs of decay. He also gives figures showing that, at any rate in one part of the county, only one child in 760 had received dental treat- ment.

One of the County Medical Inspectors reports as follows: —" During this year greater stress was laid on the condition of the teeth, with the result that a very large number of notices to parents were sent out. But it is very disappoint- ing to find that the average parent pays absolutely no attention to any advice on this point, partly through incredulity and partly air-mall carelessness. There is a fixed idea abroad that the extensive caries (decay) of the teeth is inevitable, and that decayed milk and other teeth are best left alone, as in due course they will "fall out." What really happens in the case of carious teeth is that when the disease has sufficiently advanced, the crowns of the teeth crumble away and leave the diseased fang firmly fixed in the gum where they obstruct the growth of the permanent teeth. Carious teeth ought to be either filled or extracted and never "left alone." Another Medical Inspector remarks :—" It is certain that if the children were taught the daily wie of the tooth brush at the earliest age possible a considerable amount of dental caries would be prevented, and the interest aroused in the teeth would lead to a more general resort to trea.ment for caries in its less advanced stages:" " There is still much more for the public to learn about the preservation of the teeth," says the Chief Medical Officer in summing up. He lays special stress on the fact that we give our teeth too little to do." The avoidance

of food cooked to such a degree teat it almost melts in the mouth, of pappy food, of drinking while eating, aud of finishing the meals with sugary or farinaceous foods would go a long way towards diminishing caries of the teeth. The eating of as much dry food as possible in'the dry state, of vegetables and fruit in their natural state, and the finishing of all meals with some- thing hard or dry. or with fruit, are all important factors in prevention of caries."

"I will now call Mr. George Jolley," said a solicitor at the County Court on Thursday. When "Mr. " Jolley entered the box he was found to be a boy of thirteen! *

Councillor Dr Joseph enters to-day upon his third year of office—a year that should be memorable in many ways. There is already the possibility of a Royal visit, and just a chance that Warrington Bridge may be completed. Either of these will be an event of the first magnitude in the history of Warrington. We hope His Worship will have the health and strength necessary to carry out the arduous programme of official and private duties which he has undertaken so light heartedly. _ _

The Criminal Law- Amendment Bill to deal with the White Slave Traffic is waxing stronger and stronger in its passage through the House. In addition to retaining the crucial Clause I. the Government have accepted an amendment to ensure the punish- ment of women as well as men, who live on the proceeds of immorality. The only variation in the degree of their punishment is that the women will escape the flogging which all such traffickers in vice richly deserve. *

Preaching the Citizen's Sunday sermon at Lymm the Rev. W. Bracecamp advanced some peculiar Christian teaching. He endorsed Lord Roberts' speech on the ques- tion of this country being unprepared for war and said it was his belief that the only way to secure peace was to be ready for war. The rev, gentleman is entitled to his belief, but we do not think the pulpit is the place for the advocacy of militarism, any more than we think such teaching to be on all fours with the teachings of the Prince of Peace. * o _ *

The question whether the national con- sumption of beer is increasing is raised by the report of the Commissioners of Customs and Excise issued this week. The principal increases in Customs and Excise revenue for the year 1911-12 are shown to be :

Tobacco £278,000 Tea 230,000 11tor Spirit Sugar 98,000 Beer Liquor licenses and club duty 437,000 Wine,on the other hand, showed a decrease of over £148,000. One noteworthy feature, state the Com- missioners, was the heavy yield of the beer and spirit duties. The Budget Estimate, assumed that in both cases the tendency of consumption to decline which had prevailed for about ten years would continue. In the case of beer the larger receipts appear to be partly due to the hot weather, and in the case of spirits there is reason to believe that the clearances somewhat exceeded consumption. But after reasonable allowances have been made for these factors there can be no doubt that the actual cousumption in 1911-12 showed a marked rise following on a slighter rise in 1910-11. It remains to be seen whether this indicates a change in the general trend of consumption."

The annval report of the National Liberal Federation, to be presented to the meeting of the Council in Nottingham on November 21, reviews most encouragingly the recent achievements and future prospects of the Government and the party. The discussion of the question of international relations is given effect in a resolution that will be moved at the meeting, urging that while the friendly relations between this country and France are continued, the Government will make it clear that no understanding or inten- tion is thereby implied as to military or naval action against any other Power, and expressing a strong desire that equally friendly relations on a similar footing shall be brought about between Germany and this country.

Warrington Photographic Society. , For the fourth time in successive years Mr E Seymour of London, the doyen of flower photo- graphers, delighted his audience (fifty this time) at • the Academy rooms, with his talk about flowers, wild , and tame, and how to picture them with a camera. Mr Seymour assures us that this is the easiest f the Phases of photographic work, but adds the important Tialification •• w hen you get the hang of it. To ' judge by the 120 slides shown on Thursday evening ':• Mr Seymour has got it, for nothing could excel in beauty of line and texture his studies of all our favour- . ite garden flowers. The wild species, however, were it, special featare, for the lecture was entitled " Wood- • laud and Wayside Flowers." Such subjects as the huck-beau flower, and the fluffy seeds of old man's , beard, and the willow herb showed to perfection his 37stem of lighting his studies, while the arrangement of the leaves and nuts of the hazel, the flowers of the , horse-chestnut, and the white dead nettle were ' valuable also as studies in the art of composition. A. full exposure in a softened or subdued light, and • usually without screen, is the key to success in floral rieidering, his celebrated picture of purple grapes and •'..Towtown pippins having received an exposure of 24 intinutes, so subdued waa the lighting.—A vote of thanks, moved by Mr F V L Mathias and seconded by F Daintith, was heartily accorded to the lecturer.

T. HELENS CaURCHPEOPLE AND FOREIGN

MISSIONS. The yearly meeting of the St Helens Association of the Church Missionary Society was held on Monday at ;(ke Town Hall. when the Rev Albert Baines (vicar of t Helens) presided over a large attendance. , Mr. Hugh Gamble the treasurer) reported that £520 tutd been subscribed and collected during the year by tile various churches in the district, and £505 bad been ternitted to tbe society, agalntt £761 in the previous ear. The Examiner, Saturday, NOvember 9, 1912.

NEIGHBOURS "AT WAR."

CLERGYMAN'S WIFE BRINGS COUNTY COURT ACTION.

Comedy of a Doorway.

A pecular case affecting a right of way was heard at the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Stanger, K.C., when Mrs Sarah Emily Shirtcliffe wife of the Rev F C Shirtcliff (curate of St. Ann's Church), 74, Bewsey-street, sued Mrs Jane Holland, 1, Froghall-lane, for an injunction and damages for having intercepted the free passage of plaintiff and persons authorised by her over a right of way to her property. Mr A Browne appeared for the plaintiff and Mr T S Steel represented the defendant. Mr Browne explained that Mrs Shirtcliff Was the owner of the house, 74, Beweey-street, which she pur- chased by an assignment on December 15th, 1011. At the same time the defendant purchased the adjoining house, 1, Froghall-lane, the laud affected being under one original lease. Before the properties were separated there was always a back passage , leading from the rear of the house in Bewsey- street, past the defendant's back premises into Froghall- lane. A right of way over the passige,for all tenantry purpose was given to the plaintiff in the deed. Mr Kirkoonnel, of Messrs Robert Davies and Co., acted for both parties when tee purchasers were arranged. There was a gate at the Froghall-lane end of the passage and at first it was only oolted from the inside. That arrangement having proved inconvenient, Mr Kirk connel intervened between the parties and a drop Mai was put on the gate, by mu ual consent. That also was found objectionabla and finally a lock was agreed to, each party to have a key. Mrs Shirtcliff told Mr Kirkconnel that she would not accept the key unless the door was kept unlocked the whole day through, but she would con- sent to its being locked at night. Matters went on smoothly until September when the annoyance began and from that time onwards it had gone worse,reaching a climax last week. As soon as Mrs Shirtcliffe unlocked the door in the day-time, Mrs Holland had it locked. , Consequently tradesmen's boys were unable to make use of the back entrance to Mrs Shirtcliffe's house and the Corporation men could not move the ashes. He (Mr Browne; visited the house at the plaintiff's request and was also prevented from getting access to the back door. Plaintiff now claimed nominal damages for obstruction and an injunction to restrain the defendant from interfering with her rights. Mr Steel submitted that the case did not come within the jurisdiction of the Court as the value of the property incident to the claim exceeded £5OO. Mrs Shirtcliff s property was worth £3OO, and the value of Mrs Holland's was £4OO. In reply to his Honour, Mr Steel admitted that the question of title was not being contested. His Honour said the subject matter of the action was the right of way, and that was not worth £5OO. The question in dispute was whether a trespass had been committed and he should be surprised to hesr that such an action could not be heard in the County Court, because two properties were of a total value of over £5OO. Eventually his Honour ruled against the objection and the case was proceeded with. Mrs Shirtcliff was called and bore out Mr Browne's statement. She said that she had suffered intolerable nuisance by the door being constantly locked in the day time. Since a solicitor's letter was sent to the defendant on September 25th, the door had been found locked as many as 22 times in one day. l'eople could not be heard knockiag at the door Defendant kept four dogs, and they were howling most of the time. (Laughter). In answer to Mr Steel, plaintiff said the property stood at the junction of three important roads. Mr Steel : One of them leads to the Workhouse. Mr Browne : Don't all roads lead there ? (Laughter). Plaintiff : I have not been there I,tely. (Laughter). Mr Steel : Du not tramps pass along the roadway to the Workhouse ?

_ Plaintiff: I have never seen any. Further questioned, the plaintiff said she WAS accus- tomed to pass the door almost daily twenty years ago and had never seen it locked. Mr Steel : I put it to you that you,your husband and your son have deliberately lett the door open ? Plaintiff : We have not. Asked whether she had seen her husband putting soap into the lock, plaintiff said she had not. She added, however, that she herself put sticks and loop into the old night latch to keep it open. She dented that she had objected, from the commencement, to having a key to the door. Have you ever desired to get on friendly terms with Mrs Holland ?—Yes, to prevent all this bother, when I entered the property I asked Mrs Holland if she would sell me a small strip of land against the Froghall-lane house and then I would go haves in building a party wall and remove my door to the bottom of the yard. Wasn't it you who would not have a passage unless they paid for the whole of it ?—No. George Jolley (13), 16, Allen street, spoke to Onding the back door locked on many occasions wt .en he weut to deliver milk. Mr Steel: There is no contention about the door being looked. We say we have a right to lock it. Addressing his Honour for the defenclant,Nlr Steel sold the arrangement made by Mr Kirsconnel was that each party should have a key and that the door should be locked during the day-time. There could be no object in having keys if the door was only to be locked at night. Mrs Shirtcliff had not made any endeavour to live on friendly terms with her neighbourand co-owner. On the grounds of reasonableness, it was imperative that the door should be closed during the day-time. Mrs Holland and her daughter had found innumerable tramps in their grounds I and goods had been stolen. Children had also teen in the habit of running into the garden wbeu the door I was left open by Mr and Mrs Shirtcliff. If Mrs Shirtcliff would be reasonable, there would be no diffi- culty about living peaceably together and the defendant would open the door whenever there was a knock and people wanted to get to the plaintiff a house. Mrs Shirtcliff declined to hold any conversation with Mrs Holland or to communicate with her. She had taken the law into her own hands. Mr Steel further submitted that there was no legal injury but only inconvenience at the most, and therefore it was not a case for damages or an injunction. His Honour : Inconvenience is ground for damages. Plaintiff was questioned by his Honour as to the possibility of an amicable arrangement to avoid unpleasantness in the future, but (airs Suirtclift said sh could not suggest any course that would be satisfactory to both parties. His Honour : I don't like neighbours to live side by side and to be constantly at war with each other. Plaintiff: We are not at war, your Honour. Miss Kate Holland, daughter of the defendant (who is 76 years of age) was called, and said the understand- ing was that the door was to be kept locked. If her mother had known the door was to be kept open she would not have bought the property. His Honour: Don't your dogs protect you against tramps and objectionable characters? Witness: They make a noise when people come, but they don't frighten them. In answer to his Honour witness said she had an Irish terrier, a Pomeraniam and a retriever. His Honour: If they united their forces, they might do something. (Laughter). Mr Browne: Did you not lock my clerk in the yard last week ? Witness : Sot knowingly. Mr Browne: I put it to you that you locked him in purposely. Witness : That is news to me. John Smith, Write agent, Legh-street, said the understanding was that the door should be kept locked, each party to have a key. Mr Browne : Aren't you the cause of all this trouble?

Witness : No, I don't think I am. I think you are the cause of it. (Laughter). His Honour said his decision must be in favour of the plaintiff. He should be extremely sorry if in the future the parties were not able to live—he would not say otherwise than in a state of war, because Mrs Shirtcliff objected to that phrase. At an; rate, he was afraid they -would not be very amicable if the difficulty about the passage could not be removed. If Mrs Shirtcliff could see her way to meet any reasonable objection on the part of her neighbour, he hoped she would do so.

_ Mra Shinelift : Yes, your Honour. His Honour granted an injunction, with 40.3. damages and coats.

LOCAL WEDDING.

Cotterill— Porter.

A wedding of considerable local interest was solemnized at Grove road Wesleyan Chapel,Harrogate, the contracting parties being Mr. Andrew Cotterill, of Warrington, district superintendent of the London and Manchester Assurance Co. and Miss Ethel Maud Porter, youngest daughter of Mr. T Porter, of York House, Franklin Mount, Harrogate. The service was choral, members of the Harrogate Vocal Union being in attendance. The bride, who was given away by her father, was attired in a gown or white ivory satin, with tunic of ninon and punched lace, and trimmed with pearls. The train was also trimmed with orange blossom sprays and pearls arranged in lovers' knots. She wore an old lace veil, lout by Mrs. Dodd, and carried a sheaf of lilies, the gift of the bridegroom, as was also the shower bouquet of pink carnations carried by the bride's mother. The two bridesmaids were Miss Dorothy Metcalfe and Miss Dorothea Curtis, niece and cousin of the bride. They wore Sate Greenaway gowns of white silk, with caps to match, and carried posies of chrysanthemums. The Rev IL S Seekings was the officiating minister, and the bride's brother, Mr Harold Hall, presided at the organ. The honeymoon is being spent at Barnard Castle.

PRESENTATION TO MR. RALPH ARMITAGE.

--- Mr. Ralph Armitage, younger son of Mr and Mrs Rigby Armitage, whose marriage to Miss Gwyneld Tudor will take place at Altrinchand on the 14th inst. was on Thursday the recipient of a handsome wedding present from the employers of Messrs Armitage and Rigby Limited, Warrington. There was a natural desire on the Fart of the workpeople to recognize the interesting and happy occasion, and a collection in the mills met with a very willing response. The pre- sentation took place in the office, Mr C H Hibbert introducing a depntation . representative of every department. Mr 'l' Baxter, the oldest operative in the spinning department, acted as Chairman and gave a few reminiscences of the long connection many of the employees had had with the firm. Mr. M. MASsEY, the oldest workman, made the presentation, in a neat little speech, recalling a few pleasing anecdotes in connection with the marriage of Mr Ziba Armitage forty-five years ago. On behalf of the subscribers, he then presented to Mr Ralph Armitage a solid silver tea service. and a I.3autiful clock fitted with Whittingham and Westminster chimes and bearing the inscription :—"Presented to Mr Ralph Armitage as a token of esteem by the workpople of Cickhedge and Rodney Mills in the occasion of his marriage, Novernher 14th, 1912 " Mr. RALPH ARMITAGE, in rep'ying, said he had no idea there was such a feeling among the vvorkpeople as to prompt their kindly act. Be was sure his work in the future would be a greater plea-ore from the knowledge that such a feeling of goddwill existed towards him and they could not have found a better way in which to express it.;i He was extremely thank- ful for the presents and hoped the representatives from all departments, who were present, would couvey his thanks to their work-people. Miss Tuner:, the prospecthe bride, also expressed appreciation of the gifts which, she said, would be highly valued as evidence of the good feeling that existed. Mr. WILSON spoke with feelings of gratitude of the firm's action during the coal strike and Mr. S. R. ARMITAGE responded. Mr. LOWE, on behalf of the mill, wished Mr. Ralph Armitage and the bride-elect long life add much happiness.

THE PICTUREDROME.

A special treat is in store for Picturedrome Patrons next Monday and Tuesday, when " The great Ocean Catastrophe," a thrilling and realistic picture will be shown. The follovvilg incidents are depicted: Embarka- tion at Southampton; the great ship under weigh; sports on board; the look-out in crow's nest; the Marconi opera- tor at work, the discussion of the world's news received by wireless; ice reported; the stokehold of the ship; icebergs sighted; urgent message to the captain; his agony when he knows collision is inevitable; passengers, alarm when vessel strikes; Marconi messages for help; lifeboats taking survivors from water; explosion in stoke- hold; captain and operator remain on board, latter send- ing S.O.S. message continuously; the fate of the forgotten heroes in stokehold; the last message, " we are sinking"; operator gives up his lifebelt to save a child; captain rescuing drowning passengers and ,E eturning to his ship; the last view of "The great Catasffophe."

MR. GALLAGHER'S REMOVAL. Habitue's of the Picturedrome will be sorry to learn that the popular manager, Mr. F. Gallagher, is shortly to leave. He has been appointed manager of the big Widnes Picturedrome, under the same proprietary. During his sixteen months in Warrington, Mr. Gallagher has made a host of friends who will be sorry to lose him from their midst. His successor at Warrington uiil be 'Mr. Cooper, of the Liverpool Picturedrome.
