JUNIOR LIBERALS.

MR. A. H. CROSFIELD ON CONSCRIPTION.

Criticism of N.S.L. Proposals.

Mr A H Crosfield addressed a large and enthusiastic gathering of members of the Junior Liberal Associa- tion, of which he is the president, at the club rooms in Horsernarket-street oi Monday evening. Mr E LLovn-JoNEs, who presided, said Mr Crosfield had come to them at an opportune time, for they had some excellent municipal fights and achieved magnifi- cent results on Friday. (applause). With regard to the contests in which they were not successful, he would rather be with the defeated candidates in their defeat than with the victorious ones in' th-mr victory. (Hear, bear) The rnu iicip il wore of the borough was so important that it behoved young men to study it ' particularly with reference TO such questions hS educe- I tion and housing. Mr. A. H. ettoaFIELD, who was loudly cheered on rising to address toe meeting, expressed satisfaction at the Steady increase in the membership of the Associa- tion, and said they looked to the rising generation of Lioerals to play an honourable part, and bring to bear an elevating influence in the puolic life of the town. Touching upon the mighty and tragic, yet in another aspect glo.iuus events that were opening a new chapter in the history of Europe to-day, Mr Crosfield said they must all feels stern joy that the Cross wias triumphant in that struggle. (Applause.) Many of those present would have read about the record of misrule in the Near East, but it made one feel even more strongly on those questions to have accounts, as he had had, from men actually on the spot. He would be sorry, even at a men's meeting, to quote literally the descriptions he had heard from responsible people of the kind of atrocious work which had gone on decade after decade— the unspeakable horrors, the calculated. cold-blooded agony which thousands upon thousands of Christians in that part of the world had had to suffer. And all the time the powers of Europe had looked cynically on and never lifted a finger to stop it. Year after year this appalling suffering had gone on, and still the Powers would not forget their wretched jealousies and agree that this outrageous abnegation of all humanity and of civilisation itself must be put an end to, However, they could all be thankful that the nailer powers, Greece and the Balkan States, had sunk their differences, and thanks to the high statesman-hip of the Greek Prime Minister, MI Venizelos, cut the knot and solved the problem themselves, and taught the larger Powers a lesson in wisely con- certed action which they would (II well to take to heart themselves. They might be thankful that at any rate the disgraceful attempts which some Tories had made to represent the Turks as our allies, and which were heard not many days since, were heard no more, land that on all sides and among all the nations it was now recognised that the Balk in confederation not only intended to keep the territor they had so splendidly won; but in the interests silks of justice, fair ploy and civilisation deserved every ino a of it. (Hear, hear.) Turtling to other parts of Europe nearer nome, there were signs that, having got rid of the extreme ill- feeling and tension which only a few years age existed between France and Englund, we were on the high road towards the establishment of better and more sensible relations between Germany and ourselves. (Applause.) Was that a time woee it was wise or politic,or in accordance even with the most elementary conceptions of statesin inship, for a man in so respon- sible a pesition to gJ to ftianches-,er anl speak as he was sorry to say

===== 0002601_19121109_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
LORD ROB ERTS

had spoken ? He had not heard, and personally he would be the very last to utter One syllable that would seem to indicate failure to realise and appreciate the splendid services which, in his career HS a Soldier, Lord Ronerts bail rendered to the nation. (Hear, hea-.) But while they admired and respeeted his brilliant record as a soldier, they neither could nor would folio him as a statesman. They deprecated most strongly, and they emphatically repudiated his most unfortunate, most ill-advised remarks on Anglo-German relations and policy. There were members of the National Service Lague who had made it clear that they strongly disapproved of those remarks, but he could not shut his eyes to the fact that what they had heard in Warrington had a good deal of the ring of this speech in Manchester. In the first place there had been the same talk of British unpreparedness—a pretty cool and utterly unwarranted assumption. National Service Leaguers spoke of Britain being unprepared just because we were not prepared in the way that they—the members of the League—thought best. The total annual expenditure on the defence of the entire British Empire was well over 100,000,000 sterling per annum. Mat did we get for it? Well to begin with we had an army for over-sea service which was far and away ahead of any other force of the kind in the world. We hal also the Territorials. and it was recognised by competent authorities in the country that with a stiffening of regulars they were perfectly capable of dealing with any kind of raid that was con- ceivable as long HS we retained command of the seas— and we were all agreed about the paramount necessity of that command, maintained as it was at such a tremendous cost. (Hear, hear.) Much as he deplored that enormous amount of unproductive expenditure he believed it to be absolutely necessary, and voted for it when he gas in the House of Commons. (Applause.) He had it from the highest authorities that never had our Navy, both as regards ships and personnel, been better equipped or in finer condition. They must remember that our sailors, our Regular and our Territ )rial soldiers had their hearts id their work —(hear, hear)—and took a pride in it, and it never seemed to occur to members of the National Service League that that was a great asset, and that just in se far as they seemed prepared to risk its loss they would be taking very great risks. (Hear, hear.) Then again members of the League even assume.' that the country might be denuded of every single Regular before there had been time for the Territorials to receive extra training. That was only another example Of the MILITARISTS EXTREME AND EXTRAVAGANT WAY Of looking at thinge. tie wi•-hed to draw their attention to some very significant and very important remarks, made only the other day by the German Ambassador just as he was about to take up his duties in England. The German Ambassador had referred to Britain's supremacy on the water. saying that Germany recog- nised that we possessed that supremacy, and needed it and intended to keep it. (Applause ) No doubt Germany might have jibeed a little at our laying down the law in a blunt British fashion, as we did when the Prime Minister declared in the House of Commons that we must and would retain our unchallenged and unchallengable suoremacy on the sea, hut, their Ambassador said, there was no quarrel with England on that score. (Applause.) As a consequence of our Naval supremacy let them remember that Germany's enormous Mercantile Marine was very largely at our mercy; but thank goodness the Germans could face that fact with- out troubling themselves, and with the certainty that the mass of English people were not quite so mad as to rush into a Continental struegle if by any reasonable means under Heaven it could be avoided. (Applause.) But if, having this vast pnwer on the high sas we were going to turn England into an entire nation in arms besides, what could it mean? It would mean that not only should we have command of the sea but with an enormous Mer- cantile Marine, protected by an invincible Navy, we should in fact be able iu the last resort, and in the event of the long talked of Armageddon, to ship hundreds of thousands of menet men pretty well wherever we chose. That was Lord Roberts' policy, and they had heard the same thing advocated in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) He believed the idea in the minds of many advocates of that extreme Policy was that Germany would then knuckle under and say -"it is no use for us to attempt any longer to compete with Britain," and that struck him as THE LANGUAGE AND THE IDEA OF THE :MILITARY

===== 0002601_19121109_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
GAMBLER.

(Hear, hear.) He W4S---C—eW;:lo- on the contrary that it was just that kind of language and feeling and policy that pls3ed straight into the hands of the same psrty f extreme militarism in Germany—because of course fire-eaters were not bred only in England. (Liughter ) They had to consider the effect of such a policy not merely as an ultimate menace of war, but as leading to a frightful increase of expenditure in time of peace. (Hear, hear.) Anglo- German rivalry had already led both nations into an enormous increase in Naval expenditure. Were they to go on adding millions, end tens of millions on both aides? Both Britain and Germany could find a vast deal better use for the money. (Cheers.) They had only to look at the congested parts of the cities and towns of England to realise that the housing problem was still to be reckoned with, and much also remained to be done in educational and other spheres, and in giving the poorest part of their population a chance to raise their standard of living. While attending the Conference of the International ' Association for Labour Legislation at Zurich he had ' the pleasure of discussing the question of Compulsory Service with Professor Brentano. an eminent German economist and statistician. In the course of a letter be had aince received the Professor wrote: "The intro- duction of Compulsory Service in England would not only mean a heavy financial burden for the English people but would at the same time be an enormous hindrance for English industry." Further on in his letter the Professor wrote as follows: " The indirect effects are far more important than the direct effects of Compulsory Military Service. The entire civil life becomes permeated by military considerations. It is not in the interest of liberty, and has not, on the other hand, prevented the growth of social democracy." He (Mr Crosfield) thought we had reason to think twice before we risked those indirect effects of an extreme development of militarism in England. (Hear, hear), He for one deprecated carrying militarism to that "tents because he believed it was calculated to have a hardening effect upon opinion in different ranks of society. Among the monied classes who supported compulsory service there was an idea that it would be a good thing to drill the democracy, and some of them were thinking of keeping the democracy more or less

under their thumb. (Hear, bear). Dealing with supporters of the scheme at, the other extreme, they had Mr Quelch coming to Warrington as a Social democrat and saying, in case of any little difference between employer and employed, what an excellent thing it would be if the working men had got their muskets handy and could march into the office and demand their terms at ae point of the bayonet. (Laughter.) It was a most delightful example of; extremes meeting. (Laughter.) Many employers of labour would argue that, as Professor Brawn°. put it, "Compulsory Military Service has been useful in educating our people to regularity and order." There was no doubt that regimentation was carried further in Germany than in this country, and nine out of ten Germans who had had a taste of Old 'Eneland thought it was such a comfort to enjoy British individual liberty. (Applause.) He (Mr. Crostield) entirely joined issue with tin sa members of the National Service League who maintained that we were an undisciplined nation. Industrial England was a mass of discipline, and no matter under what system we lived—whether it was State Sochlism, pure individualism' or qualified individu-dism—we could not get along without it. But there was degree and venve of proportion in all these things. Ile anew people of moderate views were supporting the principle of Compulsory Service for very high-minded reasons, bus the backbone of the movement was pretty ranch the same group of politicians in the House of Commons, and the same section of political thought in the country that was at present going in for the reokless, ill-thought out, pre- posterous attempt to introduce the old system of food taxes. In the House of Commons. so far as his infor- ruatien went, the two groups went hand in hand, and the moderate section who were supporting Compulsory Service would flud themselves swept off their feet once the dominant section got the upper hand. He noticed that his old friend M. FROST COMPLAINED OF THIS BEING A PARTY QUESTION. . _

Well to some extent it had become a Party question already. Because already it was well known in the House of Commons that the overwhelming majority of Liberals and of the Labour Party would not go in for Conscription. There war a majority on the Conservative side which was prepared to do so. Conscription had never been treated so far as a question of practical politics, but he felt certain that if it ever did reach that stage it would be almost as big a Party issue as the great Free Trade con- troversy itself. (Applaase ) Speaking of Free Trede he would like to give them another quotation from the same letter. Professor Brentano had given some strik- ing figures showing that the taxation of wheat, barley and rye in Germany enhanced the price to such an extent that the consumers paid £15,000,000 extra per annum for them. Of that sum only £5,000,000 found its way into the German Exchequer, the balance going into private pockets. He asked Professor Brentano if there had been any attempt in Germany to controvert his facts and figures, and the Professor replied: "The data which I have given in my book on the German Corn Duties have never been challenged by anybody. They are taken from official statistics; you can rely upon them absolutely." With such facts before their eyes he did not wonder that Members of the Tory Party in these days were fighting extremely shy of food taxes. (Hear, hear.) It MB one of the most serious euestions that our democracy could possibly be o.reatened with. At several recent meetings in Warrington he had referred to the magnificent legislative record of the present Government, but if we allowed tbe Protectionist influ- ence to get the upper band it would go far to nullity all, if not more than all, of that pro- gressive work. Therefore it behoved Liberals not to allow their arm mr to rust. tie did not believe the Protectionists could win on a frontal attack ; their cause was so radically rotten. Their game would be to get in on some side issue and then use their power to impose Protection on the nation. Like the Bulgarians in the Balkans ulie progressives, while they pressed steadily and persistently forward, must not allow their fl rrik to be turned ; and so their bloodless victories would be no less permanent and glorious than those achieved at such fearful cost on the battlefield. (Loud cheers,) The CHAIRMAN said that when Mr Crosfield again appealed to the electors of Warrington, ho would have I no more enthusiastic or intelligent supporters than the Junior Liberals. (Applause). Mr G TOULMIN proposed a vote of thanks to Mr Crosfleld. With regard to the municipal elections, they had discovered where the weak spots were and would in future contests be able to concentrate their forces in those wards. Touching upon war and armaments, the speaker said it had been a very serious question with many Liberals of late. There seemed to be an opinion that the Liberal party was hardly acting no to its principles on that question. He was glad that Mr. Crostield held such strong views on it, because he (Lae speaker) believed that war, in all its phases, was bad for the democracy. In future the fight for supremacy would not be one of Dreadnoughts, but of brains and bard work. (Applause). Mr. P. H. BROWN seconded and the vote was heartily passed. Mr CROSFIRLD, who was cher,red on rising to reply, said it was remarkable that any young man of intelli- gence, taking an impartial view of the situation, should fail to ally himself with the progressive cause. The work that was being done by the present Government was being followed with interest and satisfaction by all the great democracies of Europe and the United States. It was no small feather in the caps of the Liberals of England that some of the most important items adopted by ex-President Roosevelt in his endeavour to reform the Reonblican party of America, were taken direct from their programme or from their accomplished work. (Applause.) As far as Parlia- mentary representation was concerned, Warrington stood for

CONCERT AT STOCKTON HEATH.

The Church Institute, Stockton Heath, was filled with an unusually appreciative audience on Thursday evening, the attractiam being a conceit promoted by Mrs C Garton Stone in aid of the funds of the Stockton Heath Garden Society. In the arrangement of a most interesting programme, Mrs. Stone had happily enlisted the services of members of the Warrington Musical Society, who were responsible for the whole of the vocal items. Of Mr. J. Rabbitt's interpretations of "Come into the Garden, Maud " and " Dear Heart" it need only be said that they were fully in keeping with his reputation for artistic and soulful singing. He invested them with ardour and tender- ness and so delighted the audience that demands for recalls were unmistakably insistent, Mr. Babbitt responding with "Two Eyes of Grey "and " Myra." Madame Lily Fairhurst also proved a favourite, her singing of "My Ships " and "Come sing to me " being marked by excellent enunciation and all the necessary feeling and expressiveness. She too was twice encored, and was also eminently successful in " Roses " and "Home sweet Home. " Miss Mullins did not fully realize the plaintive spirit of that pleasing trifle " Absent " and, although she sang sweetly, her rendering was not at all moving and gave one the impression that she was not happy in her choice of song. Mr. J. Poole entered into the rousing old English lilt of "Sang of the Bow "and "Drake goes west" with fine verve and freedom, earning general appreciation which was loudly expressed. The performance of two excerpts from " Dorothy,"—" Be wise in time" and "You swear to be good and true"— in which Mrs. Stone and the vocalists already mentioned were associated, served only to confirm one's original view that the balance of voices was herefar from satisfactory. However, the captivating melody and bri,:tht by-play of the quartet ensured its popularity, and a repetition had to be conceded. Mr W Moores, as a peppery officer in spick and span military uniform, created immense amusement with "My word," and later gave his last season's favourite, "Captain Gingah," and "Mary Ann, she's after me," with equal acceptance. Mr Harvey Adams' sketch "Thought reading," was a clever and novel feature. The society's orchestra, under the conductorship of Mr W E Ray, played a number of selections, the most important of which .was "Tom Jones." It cannot be said that the rendering of German's fascinating music was at all adequate, for intonation and details of execu- tion were alike most disappointing, and indicated that much more thorough rehearsal is necessary before the L orchestra can hope to do justice to works of this type. L Mr Bert Smith played the various pianoforte accom- paniments with taste and judgment.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

There have been numerous skating turns on the music hall stage, but not many can claim to be as successful as that presented by Jack McLallen and May Carson at the Hippodrome this week. Nothing seems impossible to this incomparable duo and their evolutions within the limited area of the stage cannot fail to evoke the admiration of all. With ease and grace they execute the most intricate and hazardous evolu- tions, a feature of the performance being a step-dance on rollers by McLallen whilst he is mourned on a Oft. pedestal with a 2ft. dancing board. John C McMahon and Ivy Du For present a comedy duologue "The sleeping car" and although in the main the theme is a laughable ones slight modification in one or two of the situations would be more acceptable. Master David Paget, whose return visit here has been eagerly anticipated delights the audience with his skilful manipulation of the violin. His selections are highly popular and executed with that cleverness which has made him so famous. A sporty and comical sketch is presented by Tom and Nell with a large amount of success. As comedy boxers and tricksters they give us something new, clean smart and clever. Dein Castle has indeed had a warm reception and he is thoroughly deserving of it for he is a light comedian of the Lashwood order and the manner in which he gives his two catchy songs is highly commendable. Will Hay convulses the audience with his humorous songs and there is not a dry eye in the house when he has finished his "Bend down" number. R A Speedwell is exceedingly clever as " The wizard painter" aed he executes his work with lightning rapaditv. Male Dillon, the happy Dutch girl is a clever dancer and Connie Mascot is equally successfii. as a comedienne. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. Amongst the turns to appear next week are Scott and Whaley, America's premier coloured comedians ; Herr Doring and his pigeons ' • August and September, the popular comedians; It H Douglas in Types from Stageland " ; Dexter, the man who draws with both hands ; Laurie and Elsie, harmonisers and expert dancers ; Hammon and Wyatt, Sarulow girls in singing novelties ; and Edna Deno, comedienne and dancer.

CHARGE AGAINST WORKS' EMPLOYEES.

At the Warrington Police Court, yesterday, Thomas Hackett, clerk, 7, Frederick-street, Hildebrand Bench, clerk, 23, Longdin-street, and Peter Knight, jun., goods inspector, 15, Stanley-street, all employed at Messrs Richmonds, Ltd, Academy-street, were charged - with stealing a quantity of goods, the property of the company during the last six months.—The Chief Constable stated that Sergeant Reddington and Detec- tive Summers had been making inquiries into a number of thefts which had taken place at the works. A Man ' was arrested on Wednesday and brought before the court and the case was adjourned for a week. Inquiries were continued and as a result the three prisoners were arrested in connection with other thefts. A large quantity of the other property had not yet been recovered and the police had reason to believe that the three prisoners were also connected with it. In the possession of one of the prisoners were found 50 or 60 halfpenny stamps which had been affixed on something and taken off. A carter had been removing the goods from the works on the instructions of the prisoners. It was not until one o'clock that morning that Sergeant Reddington had completed enquiries and it was necessary that the case be adjourned.—The case was adjourned until next Wednesday.

An exciting game continued, Barnton forcing about four fruitless corners in five minutes. About ten minutes off time Challinor added a third. Results;— St Barnabas, 3; Barntoll. 1.

She said it was true they did not see victory yet, but it was a great thing to have taken deliberately one of those steps which meant the crossing of the boundary one of those boundaries between the stages of which every reform had to go through. And it had all come about in seven years. The Research Defence Society had altered the whole attitude of the anti-vivisection figut. There were the two forces in the field, the Anti-vivisection society and the Research Defence Society, trying to get the public ear and that meant ultimately what the anti-vivisectionists wanted namely, greater publicity. They knew they were in the right and that the right view always conquered in the end. (Hear, hear.) It was true that the Research Defence Society had privileges and favours. It got int) the public schools and the Harmsworth Press which were denied, or closed to the anti-vivisectionists. They could get even with them and would do so before long. Vivisection was regarded now as one of the great con- troversial questions of the day And that was not the case seven years ago. All that was to the advantage of the cause. (Hear, bear.) She thought it was a great thing that they had become so strong that the adversary had to come out to fight them. There had been a Royal Commission on the subject—such an extraordinary commission. It was packed with vivisectors who sat there to judge their own Actions. No anti-vivisectors were aro el to inquire about the action of any vivise::- tionist. The commission was compelled by public opinion to state that the amount of pain inflicted ought to be limited ; that the animals ought to be considered before the value of the experiment. In regard to any benefice derived from the vivisectionist's treatment of animals all that the Commissioners could any was "it is claimed," "it is stated " " Sir Victor Horsley gave it as his opinion " and we were told so but not once would they say it is so; vivisection has gained these benefits." The Research Defence Society issued leaflets on which was stated that such and such a thing had been remedied by vivisection of animals. The Anti- vivisectionists were so sure of their statements and convictions that they published the leaflets of the Research Defence Society along with their own. They were quite sure of the strength of their arguments that they gave it away bound up along with their own. (Hear, hear.) When the public compared those things and saw that on one side there was assertion and on the other side, solid argument they would come to the anti-vivisectionist view in the end if they were a little more educated. It seemed to her there was something wrong in regard to education. It was especially the case with the education of the !upper classes in the public schools that there was the dreadful tendency to accent everything that was said by what was celled authority without the least examination at all. There was so much following on conventional lines without any attempt at original thought. (Hear, hear.) It was the bounden duty of every anti-vivisectionist to educate themselves. The day was past now when anti-vivisectionists could be sentimentalists. They should stimulate their own power of tbonght and think out the matter on the moral side. In regard to the qaestion of the rights of animals, that could be summed up in 000 unanswerable phrase. "The least of all possible rights is to be spared the worst of all possible wrongs." (Hear, hear.) If animals had any rights at all they had a right to be protected against all possible wrongs. That was the anti-vivisectionist view. They ought to study also the medical arguments. It was now a matter of history that be viviseccors.and the Research Defence Society in par- ticular was unblushing in the false statements it made that this, that or the other had been discovered through vivisection. They shoulo try to find out whether such statements were true. If they would educate them- selves in that way they would be able to overcome any argument of the vivisectionist party, (Applause). On the proposition of Mr C JACKSON HOLMES it was decided to form a branch of the British 'Union of Anti- Vivisectionists.

A hearty vote of thanks to the Mayor and speaker brought a pleasant meeting to s close.

MUNICIPAL HOUSING SCHEMES. Mr. John Burns opened the Yeovil Corporation's housing scheme, one of the largest municipal schemes In the country, at Yeovil on Saturday. Speaking to several thousands of people, Mr Burns said that, as President of the Local Government Board, he felt honoured in being asked to come to Yeovil to inaugurate such a large and good scheme of house accommodation as bad been carried out under the Housing and Town Planning Act. The Yeovil Town Council bad met a real difficulty by real remedy. Iu a generous and practical way they had erected good houses, at fair rents, for decent tenants, so that they could live healthy, happy, sober, and comfortable lives. The scheme comprised 132 five-shilling houses, 10 four- shilling houses, and 16 flats at 2s Gd. When completed the estate would have accommodation for seven or eight hundred people, and for a town of the size of Yeovil this was a very large contribution to the Solu- tion of the housing problem. If the local authorities in the country followed the example of the Yeovil Council, the back of the housing problem would be broken. He congratulated the Yeovil Council on their courage, wisdom and prescience, and hoped the scheme would be an example and precursor of many more schemes in the country.

QUAINT SAYINGS OF CHILDREN. Dr. :11acnamara, M.P., addressing tho members of the South London Tabernacle Institute Literary and Debating Society, Peckton, on Saturday night, told the following stories of quaint sayings attributed to children : Teacher : " What is a mediator ?"—The Little Pupil : " Please sir, a mediator is a chap, who says ' hit me instead.'" Teacher (to a Lindon class) : " What is grass ?" Little Boy : " Grass is what you have to keep off." " What is a definition of the phrase ' etcetera " continued Dr. Macnarnara, " It is used to make people believe you know more than you do." "Why would David rather be a doorkeeper in the House of the Lord ?"—" Because he could walk outside while the sermon was being preached." "How do you know the earth is round ?',—" Because it says in the Bible world without end."

ANTI-VIVISECTION. Annual Meeting of the Warrington

Branch.

The activities of Anti-vivisectionists throughout the country have been far more pronounced recently, than ever before and such logical arguments and sensible views are put forth that one cannot fail to realise that the practice of vivisection is opposed by a stolid and determined body of men and women. The Warrington Anti-vivisection Society held its annual meeting in the Winmarleigh Cafe on Wednesday evening and althongh nothing extracrdinary was reported it vral evident that those who are members of this organi- sation had spared no effort to make the society a success. The president of the society the Mayor ,(Councillor Dr Joseph) presided over a moderate attendance and an interesting address was given by Miss Kidd of Loudon.

THE OFFICIALS. On the proposition of Councillor JEN:Kiss the follow- ing officials were elected :—Presidenn, the Mayor ; vica-presidents, Miss Bessie Dutton, the Rev A M Mitchell, Mr C Jackson Holmes ; committee,Councillor

Jenkins,'Mr and Mrs Fulton SMith, Miss O'ornall, Mrs C Jackson Holmes, Miss W A Jones, Mrs IN.leild, Miss Mullard, Mr Noble, Mr Spiegelhalter, Miss V White, sbd Mrs Willet ; treasurer, Mr C Jackson Holmes, secretary, Mrs C Jackson Holmes, organisiug secretary and lecturer, Mr It E O'Callaghan. COMMITTEE'S REPORT. The Committee's report for 1910-11 which was adopted, stated that during the past two years toe number of public meetings, lectures, addresses and debates had been materially increased and by that means they had reached social, political and Working- men's Clubs, Mutual Improvement and Debating Societies. Some 25 illustrated lectures had been given by the organising lecturer with an approximate attend- ance of 3,000. They had again devoted special attention to securing openings in young men's and young women's Bible clasees,Adult Schools,P.S.A 's and similar gather- ings swayed more especially by moral considerations. Although their task of educating the community in the principles of anti-vivisection had been kept to the fore the more concerted efforts of the committees work and general meetings had unfortunately been very much embarrassed. Tte committee had been able to carry out a large amount of work and also to close their .aceounts with a small balance in hand. The late Hon

Secretary having felt compelled to resign her position, C Jackson Holmes had once more stepped into the breach and zealously taken up her duties. In conclu- sion, the committee felt that perhaps at no period of their history had there been so much need of earnest workers. It was essential that the whole energy of the abolition forces should be bent upon defeating any further •endowment of legalised cruelty. Great forces were arrayed against them and it was incumbent upon them therefore to meet force with force—skill with skill. Given those, with uutailing courage and faith in their cause, they need have no fear as to their ultimate victory. In his opening remarks. the CHAIRMAN said he was pot very well up in the position of the anti-vivisection mnvernent. At least he believed that, during the last year or two no great victories had been gained. He regretted that and also the small attendance present that night. He thought one reason was the many other counter-attractions In the town, .and one more.particu-

larly the concert in the Parr Hall. During a lifelong observation he had in variably foundlthat lovers of music were also lovers of animals, and they were thei ones likely to be supporters and promoters of that movement of theirs, and had probably gone to the concert. (Hear, tear.) One or two things had struca him recently as to whether they were acting on the right course. Although it might be considered rather ungrammatical he could not help saying that they ought to begin at the beginning. (Hear, bear.) The question he would put before the people generally was, had the so-called lower animals any privilege of their own; had the people an undisputed right over everything ; did we owe them nothing in return ? Everything hinged on that If the people could only define the limit to which they could push their power over those creatures dependent on the human race for everything. If they could do that, then there would be something for them to work npcn. That was the thing which ought to be done first of all as it was the foundation for their movement. Might was right now as ever it was centuries ago. It might be veneered over by the laws of society or covered over by a veneer which made it not so unpleasant but nevertheless there it was. Referring to the recent meeting in connection with the White Slave Tragic movement, ,his Worship said what they wanted was to get public opinion in their favour to the extent that was manifested at the meeting at the Parr Hall. (Hear, hear.) They had had unanimous support of that actland the consequence was that the Government were forced, as it were, to give way to the popular opinion. They, as anti-vivisectionists were at present in the minority end they depended greatly on the publication of their arguments and views. In conclusion he would say that they ought to ask those who were against them whether they really thought those animals had any I rights of their own. Whether they were to be really sacrificed at our will or whether that will was properly exercised whether for use or abuse. If that question was answered then they might have some basis upon I which to work. (Hear, hear.) He would also ask those who were supporters of the anti-vivisection movement to be more bold in their belief. As an illustration of how public opinion affected a cause the Mayor said that supposing a gentleman died, mention was made of his character, and amongst his many qualities they found that he was a good sportsman, a good rider to hounds,a good shot and that kind of thing. All those were put down as good qualities. He personally did not think they were. They ought not to be mixed up with those qualities which made a man revered without some little criticism. The opening ceremony at the sale of work in Fair- field schools on t-aturday was presided over by the Rector, who said he desired to express his most grateful appreciation for all the work which had been done by the parishioners during the year. Gifts to Liverpool Cathedral.

Sir William Forwood presided at a full meeting of the Liverpool Cathedral Committee, held at the Churchi House, on Monday. The contents of the will of the late Mr. Thomas Bartlett were reported to the committee, and it WbS explained Wet it was impossible to ascertain at present what amount was likely to be received npart from the very generous sum, £7,500, given for the hells and clock ; but that, after payment of all legacies and duties, the amount of the residuary estate given up to the committee would not he considerable, and would consist, to a large extent, of reversionary interests,l which would not come into possession for many years. It was reported that a lady, who desired that her name might not appear, had promised to present the Bishop's throne to the Cathedral. Designs for the four organ cases were submitted and the consideration postponed.

The New Hall In Hanover-street. As we write (says Holy Trinity Church Magazine) the new hall is on its way to completion, and may he expected to be opened within a week or en of the pub- lication of the magazine. The Hon Misses Wilson Patten, we understand, are asking their niece, the Hon Mrs Lumley, to perform the opening ceremony ; and they are also sending out invitations to the subscribers to the Factory-yard Mission; and to the mothers who have been so long connected with it as members, and to friends and officials who have taken special interest in the work. Those who have a chance of looking inside the building are able to report that the hail is really a beautiful room. The exterior also is most pleasing, and as the building is most substantially built, it will he for many generations, we trust, a standing memorial to the kind and constant interest of the late Lord Winniarleigh's family in the district, in which, in days gone by, many of his tenantry were to be found.

Wycliffe P.S A. Society.

- - There was a fair attendance on Sunday last, Mr Jos Owen presiding. Mr W Carter gave the address on " The two wave " or "The broad and narrow way." He dwelt upon the free will of man, and his power of choice, refusual or acceptance. The broad way was full of inducements and allurements. Inducements were very disappointing, and allurements often turned out darkness instead of light, hitter instead of sweet. The narrow way was sufficiently wide for the Christian to walk therein. God's promises were for those who trod this road and never failed. The brord way led to death, the narrow way to life. Mr Mcliachern read the lesson the president led the prayer.

"A Tribute to a Working Man." The Rev H Ogden preached a sermon with this title at Golborne-street Baptist, Church on Sunday. The preacher explained that he had been led to take this subject by the passing away of an old wi.redrawer employed at Whitecross Works. He toiled until he was GS, then in a brief week he passed away. One could say much of a system that exacted toil from school to tile grave, but that was not the occasion. He was a man who made no fuss in life, but plainly did his duty. The call of work always found him at his post. When the day was over there were no wild revels for him, but a patient rearing and strengthening of his home. lie lived quietly and with reserve, but never failed when a kindre,ss was needed. A poor neighbour was never forgotton. He frequented church seldom or Dever, and yet had a quiet faith in God. He (the preacher) mentioned that fact because the deceased was a type. When a king died a goldmine was ruined to bury him. When a faithful worker died hardly a word was said. The sea envelops him, a gurgle and the end. Yet this man built God's temple. Cathedrals were nothing without worshippers. Churches were only bricks and mortar unless there was lite. Brother Grayson was an empire builder. Such as he made good clean English life possible. Be belonged to the unknown crowd. The stones be laid in God's temple were marked in red. His life was touched with the Blood of the Cross.

"Religion and Social Life."

Dr. Mellor's Indictment of Modern Society. Cairo-street Chapel was again crowded on Sunday evening when the Rev Dr Stanley Mellor continued his course of sermons on Religion and Democracy." The special subject under consideration was " Religion and social life," and the preacher at the outset ieferred to the lethargic, indifferent spirit which was so largely spread abroad in the world to-day. The rich led lives of idleness and pleasure, thinking only of to-morrow, never considering what their riches meant, nor where they sprang from, nor at what a cost they were engendered. The men and women of the middle classes were enslaved to monotony and, beyond the cares of business, interested only in their wardrobes, their gardens, their families, making the spice of their existence out of scandal and gossip, mysteriously unalive to change and movement. The poorer classes, once their day's toil was behind them, for the most part had no further c, ncern with life at all, save as it offered them a little relaxation and amusement. That spirit of indifference, which permeated society from top to bottom, that refusal to be troubled by really .vital problems or interested by desperately serious issues, that unreadiness even to be alarmed about anything at all, Was One of the most discouraging things that religious idealism had to face. If they tried to insert a little paQsion into life, people for the most part regarded them as a art of nuisance, a kind of objectionable insect that ought to be crushed. Discussing our present•diy civilization, the preacher said it was essentially materialistic. Modern society was not interested in God excepting in so far as He was a "paying concern." The great words of life to-day were trade, business, commerce, industry, riches, money, wealth, and indeed the actual magnitude and magnificence of material resources and accumulations to-day were amazing to behold. At first sight it would seem that a society so amazingly rich ought to display a very beautiful and noble form of livinc, at least one would expect it to maintain a reasonable standard of comfort for all its members. But such did not seem to be tbe case. On the contrary, the rule seemed te be that in all those societies in which there was great wealth, there was also present always a hateful and hideous thing called poverty. In the midst of all those societies in which wealth was so enormous there was always present a not inoonsideeable number of men, women and children who were either actually without material resources altogether, starving or. kept from starvation only by the working of a machine which was known as private or public charity. And when one investigated this state of affairs, this horrifying contrast, further, one was faced by an extraordinary revelation. These modern societies, it seemed, in which the actual wealth WhS so prodigious were all organised, if one might use the term, on a certain well defined system, the most characteristic feature in which was the method by which wealth was distributed. All wealth was the product of labour; wherever there was wealth it meant that somewhere work had been done to produce it. To our point of view, the normal and natural thing woeld be that the members of a society should share in the wealth of that society in proportion to the labour expended by each on its production.. But that simple and fair method was despised by civilized nations so completely that they had invented another method, totally different, whereby wealth, as fast as it was created, passed into the hands of a very few members of the community who then doled out to the rest, the vast majority, only so much as seemed necessary to keep body and soul together for the production of still more wealth. The wealth that went into the hands of the few was known as profit, interest, rent,and

such like, and helped to swell the amount of what was called invested cipiral, whilst the little portion of wealth that was doled out to the many was called wages,:and by those who supported the system was sup- posed to be a fair return for the work done, much of that work, as was clearly evident, being arduous and exhausting, and some of it actually degrading to the bodies and minds of those who by force of circum- stances were compelled to undertake it. The first and most startling result of the system was to divirie all modern secieties into two very distinct cl a,es—one class at the top, very small in remicer but possessed of all the advantages and opportunities of life, called the rich; the other class at the bottom, very numerous and constantly increasing, eh-void or ail advantages and opportunities, called the poly. At the basis of the poor were the literally s,ra, visit and destitute who really constituted the feticide- jen to which these money-making societies rested between the destitute and the rich, were grades of pour and less poor, each smaller in number Lean the class below it, each grade occupied by individuals whose sole endeavour in life seemed to he to get another grade higher, all the time taking care to push those below still further down. Having strongly criticised the competitive and class systems, the preacher touched upon the monstrous principle of inheritance by which, without rhyme or reason, sense or meaning, the son of a rich man, by the mere accident of birth, was rich and the son of a poor man was poor, with the handicap of life dead nastiest him from the very start. In modern societies there was no such thing as equality of opportunity, and consequently there was no genuine justice. Modern society was nothing but exploitation—exploitation of the many by the few for the sake of gain, an exploitation which never stopped to count the cost in terms of degraded bodies, stunted intelligence, debased emotions, broken hearts and shattered lives. No fine words, no loud protestations could alter the facts of the case. Men were blinded by the system, no doubt, and deluded themselves with vain pnrases but when the mind was open and the senses were truly awake, there was no escape from the fact that modern society, by the very principle of its organisation, literally stank of selfishness, cupidity, brutality and rottenness, and appeared as fundamentally opposed to the best things in life. So long as we continued to believe that the main object in life was gain, that the aim and meaning of all labour was not use or beauty but profit, reckoned in monetary terms, so long would things remain as they were, and no amount of tampering and tinkering could alter them. The happiest thing idealism and religion could say about our present mode of living was that some day it would come to an end. But before that end could come, men must see that tbe present was really unendurable. The first step towards the ideal was the destruction of the present. That was why religion should preach ceaseless discontent and labour after revolt.

Wesley Guild Council.

Rev. H. Mudie Draper on Indifference to Religion.

The progress which has been made in all the branche of the Wesley Guild Council during the year was under review at the annual grand united rally which was held in the Latchford Wesleyan Church on Monday evening. There was a large attendance of members over which the Rev W A Beckett presided. He was supported by the Rev H ‘ludie Draper, of London, who delivered a most inspiring addres3, and the Rev E I\l organ. At the commencement the Chairman said he hoped " forward " would be their watchword and motto throughout the whole session which lay before them. They had a noble cause to work for and one which was worthy of their highest efforts. (Hear, hear). The Secretaries of the various branches having sub- mitted their reports, which all showed steady progress, the Chairman moved their adoption and remarked that they were very satisfactpry and showed that good work had been done. (Hear. hear.) They would all be glad to know that their Ellesmere-street friends had com- menced a Guild and they had SS to 40 members, of whom seven-eighths were active members. (Applause.) The reports were then adopted. The Rev, H. Media Draper, who introduced an accept- able smattering of humour in his speech and brought his points home by homely sentiment, !mid it seemed to him that the young people were being considered far more to-day than ever before. Things were greatly changed, for in the olden days children were not con- sidered at all. They were supposed t 6 have no voice, and no particular work in the church. When they had passed the Sunday school it was thought that the children could be left alone ,until they became old enough to be Church members. The Wesley Guild and other institutions had come into existence because the Church recognised that there was a work the children could do. No municipality could occupy a model posi- tion to-day unless it provided reading and recreation rooms for the young men and women of the town. (Hear, hear.) A Bill dealing with the training of young people in the schools bad been discussed for many weeks by Parliament, and such a thing as that would have been impossible 50 years ago. One of the great features of present day life, whether social, national or Church, was that the young people were being recognised, and it was seen that they had an important part to play in life of every kind. If by chance the elders did overlook the children, the young people soon showed that they were not going to be overlooked. He would warn the children not to get " swelled head " because they were being made so much of. They were

FEELING TIRED?

MAN in his primeval state lived a natural outdoor life, exercising equally all the muscles and organs of his body, eating to satisfy a hunger derived:from his continuous battle with the elements. If he wanted medicine he had recourse to the herbs growing around him. Hence he was always in a perfect state of health and vigour.

To-day he shuts himself for hours in the vitiated atmosphere of a workshop, factory or office. He takes little or no exercise. Food it swallowed to satisfy a hunger engendered by habit and not by natural requirement. He soon becomes a victim to nervous depres- sion, lowered vitality and chronic debility. The first symptoms are simple—he complains of always feeling tired. Distrust that tired feeliftg, it may be the forerunner of very serious illrrOss.

THE MEANING OF XXX ALES. or' was wondering the other day how many my readers know the meaning of the I)voi.Pular XXX mark put upon all barrels. e arrington boys and girls see it often _llOllOl, and when they go to Liverpool they 7_o blazoned over numberless public-houses " legend about famous Warrington XXX al—

These crosses are very interesting, though te.rhape the brewers themselves don't know Ze moaning of them. They take us back to ..48 time when ale was made in the I"onasteries by the monks. They had the rt of ale brewing, and supplied their liquids Royalty. The strong ale (probably that •,`Q.own as " doble doble ") loved by Queen I,hzabeth and Mary Queen of Scots was nonastery brewed. Of course there were all sorts of inferior .18.nds put on the market and in order to 'lB convincing proof of quality the monks Ised to swear over each barrel that it was 10,°d stuff fit to drink, and in token of the 4111 they put the sign of the cross. Later J' they gave extra assurance by sending out e that was double sworn. The XXX mark rle later and meant that they had sworn ree times by the sign of the cross that it as good stuff. The crosses became in process of time a lotrt of trade mark and they are still used in "'US way.

LOLLYPOP LAND.

Close your eyes, little boy. And take hold of my hand Then you may go with me To Lollypop Land, Where peppermint canes Are the trunks of trees, And chocolate cigars Are the branches, with leaves Of golden brown taffy. There's every known brand For children to chose from In Lollypop Land. The houses are fashioned Of caramel bricks, With rafters and beams Made of cinnamon sticks, There's an ocean with waves Of vanilla ice cream, And big soda fountains That give out a stream, With glasses and spoons You may feast on the sand That's formed of white sugar In Lollypop Land. Every flower that grows Is a bright candy drop, With marshmallow lilies And almonds a crop. Of jawbreakers striped To roll on on the grass, That's made of spun sugar , And sparkles like glass. And everything sweet Will be yours to command, Sa jump in the dream-boat For Lollypop Land. —lrene Elliott Benson. THIS WEEK'S STORY.

IF THE LABOUR PARTY DID.

[BY T.L.]

" Women's Votes." " Expected Defeat in the Commons." Such were the headlines with which the long-expected and much-discussed Labour newspaper startled the public in its first issue. Such was its first pronouncement on the great question which is to come forward in Parliament at no distant date. It was not a cheerful pronouncement, and the " Daily Citizen " opined that the Government's difficulty was likely to cause a breakdown of the Reform Bill ; that the Speaker may rule that the Bill, deprived of its chief clauses, must be dropped for technical reasons ; and that the Plural Voting Bill will be substituted for it. .

'This disquieting news was not without its result. Miss Christabel Paukhurst announced in "Votes for Women " that the new Labour newspaper had declared for the great betrayal. " Women are to be cheated of the vote with the connivance of the Labour Party." Once more, therefore, she called upon her supporters to rally to the W.S.P. U. policy, militant methods; voting against the Government on every occasion until a women's franchise amendment is made part of the official Liberal programme. Here, then, we have two of the three important factors in the situation. The attitude of the Labour Party and the question of Militant Methods are undoubtedly at present in the forefront, they are furthermore intimately connected, for it is to those who favour militancy outside the House who are loudest in their support of the policy we have referred to with regard to the Government. The third factor is the Nationalist vote: but this has not yet forced itself on our atten- tion in a critical manner, since the defeat of the Conciliation Bill last march. Nevertheless, it is a problem to be faced and the" Manchester Guardian " rightly pointed out the other day that "the Nationalist members probably now hold the balance in their hands." The connection of the Nationalist attitude with the Labour party is clear, for in ranging them- selves as a party or even as individuals against the Women's Cause, Nationalists would court the future opposition and hostility of all the democratic forces in this country ; and first and foremost, as Mr Snowden has pointed out, they would alienate the good will of the Labour party. We are, however, not here concerned so much with the Nationalist vote, important as it may be, for many things may happen in the near future to deter- mine the direction in which it may be influenced, moreover, it is well-known that negotiations have been opened up between the Labour and Nationalist parties on this very question. The most important question of the moment is the policy to be adopted by the Labour Party. They are urged to oppose the Govern- ment on every occasion. Mr Lansbury has publicly declared for this policy. What would happen if the Labour party did? That this question has already presented itself to the leaders of the Party is clear from many indica- tions and the ' Daily Citizen" was not long in follow- ing up its despondent pronouncement considered above, by a leader on the Women's Cause." "The • Daily Citizen" rightly insists that it is beyond ques- tion the women's movement which has forced to the front every phase of electoral reform, and it is emphatic that men's suffrage is not a question that can now be shirked. It then points to certain con- siderations which we think cannot be too strongly urged against those who regard these tactics as practical politics. In the first place it makes it clear that the Labour Party is, after all, primarily a " Labour" party. " How many converts to women's suffi age would be won among the miners if the Labour Party voted against urgent miners' questions on the ground that the policy of the Government on the stiff rage was unsatisfactory ?" Yet it is chiefly to represent the interest of the miners that the miners representatives are at present in Parliament. secondly, even if we suppose these methods success- ful, what would be gained? It is as clear that the Government will never be forced to adopt a demo- cratic measure against its will, as that the Labour party will never as a party oppose measures which are in the interests of Labour. Yet for the sake of argu- ment let U 3 suppose that the tactics in question gained the approval of the leaders of labour. The Govern- ment goes out. Enter Mr Austen Chamberlain, kfr F E Smith and Lord Cromer—we had almost said Lord Curzon. Surely the very ment;on cf Mr Austenl Chamberlain is sufficient to. show the futility of the policy in question. Such lacing the case, we must agree with the " Daily Citizen," and with most of the more far-sighted pro- gressive politicians of the day. We must reject these counsels of despair and concentrate the Liberal and Labour forces on one point. That point is the Adult Suffrage Amendment to the Government Bill. For its own sake, and for the sake of women, the Labour Party cannot run the risk of a Conservative adminis- tration. And its p inciples compel it to endeavour to obtain the largest possible measure of women's enfranchisement, namely, Womanhood Suffrage. If Liberals would but recognise this, and art up to the ideals which they themselves profess, there should be little difficulty in making the present opportunity a turning-point in English history. ; :` Labour, said Air Arthur Henderson a few dais ago " stands first and foremost for the rights of citizen- ship for every man and wc man, recognising that the franchise is an essential element of representative government." Such is tile position of Labcur. Whether Mr Asquith's undertaking be fulfilled or not," says the " Manchester Guardian," one thing is clear. No extension of the suffrage would be tolerable which did not include women ; and if the Labour party stand firm—as we believe it will stand—none will he possible." Liberalism and Labour are, then, really at One in this matter, and no measure upon which Liberalism and Labour are united at thelpresent time can fail to become law. It is in this spirit that propaganda must proceed during the coming critical weeks. Above all we must deprecate any further out- bursts of militancy. Those who in the past were not against militant tactics, and who even supported them with enthusiasm, must come to see clearly that at the present time they are both futile and pernicious. The situation calls for steady and considered action. It is essentially a political situation' and one demanding an accurate judgment on political possibilities. If the ideal of universal suffrage for both men and women is now at last to be attained, it will only be by making use of the facilities which Mr. Asquith's undertaking has placed at our disposal. Matters have gone too far for the pledge which the Prime Minister has given to be nullified by any normal course which events may take. But supposing Mr. Asquith capable , of such a mode of action as his opponents imagine, then their methods are precisely those calculated to I reach the end which they wish to avoid. Given a fair chance, given fair play, given adequate enthusiasm, we can yet be successful. Such is our analysis of the position and such are the reasons which lead us to conclude that the Labour Party would be in the highest degree ill-advised to vote against the Government. until the Reform Bill is before the House.

WARRINGTON NONCONFORMITY.,

ITS 250th ZINNIVERSARY.

DR. ENFIELD AND

The choice of a successor to Mr. Seddon must have seemed a momentous one to those upon whom the responsibility rested. The pulpit of the chapel had assumed greater importance now that tutors and scholars foregathered there, and Mr. Seddon by his unremitting labours and by the position he had gained in the Academy, had left a gap most difficult to fill. ,

===== 0002601_19121109_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
A VERSATILE SCHOLAR.

Yet all the intellectual demands were met in the scholar to whom an invitation was given,and William Enfield came to Warring- to be both Minister and Rector Acadenra. As though the honours and attendant toils were not enough, he became also tutor in Belles Lettres. Two years more and the versatile doctor had added the mathe- matical department to his other respon- sibilities, and as the "provider of commons" had resigned he cheerfully took charge of the commissariat also. There was really no getting to the end of the willing doctor's powers. Until the Academy closed its doors Dr. Enfield, was its devoted servant. Between the years 1774 and 1773 he and Dr. Aikin managed all its affairs and conducted practi- cally all its classes, a cluster of labours that must have rested heavily even though they,

===== 0002601_19121109_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. WAKEFIELD.

' Dr. Nicholas Clayton had ministered in Liverpool and won golden opinions by the beauty and originality of his sermons. It was an unhappy choice that brought him to Warrington in the declining years of the Academy for the depressing experiences left a mark upon his health and spirits from which he does not appear to have recovered.

===== 0002601_19121109_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. GILBERT WAKEFIELD.

Of a very different order was Dr. Gilbert Wakefield, the new teacher of classics. This fine scholar holds a distinct place in the history of English learning and next after Priestley his name is the most famous of those that adorn the history of the War- rington Acadt my. A few lines concerning his career and attainment will give to our readers some idea of the remarkable man who took up his abode in Bewsey-street in 1781. He was born February 22nd 1756 in the parsonage house of St. Nicholas, Nottingham, his father being the rector of that parish. After a distinguished University career in which he won the highest honours in ' Classics he was ordained in his 22nd year a deacon in the Church of England. Several months were spent as curate in Stockport, followed by several months in Liverpool. He began here to reveal the courage and beliefs

which were to mark his career to its end. He tried to stir opinion against the Slave Trade, and visited almost daily the prisoners brought in by the privateers. His studies led him to adopt Unitarian views upon which he resigned his position in the Church of England. In this position and at the age of 25 the invitation from the Academy Trustees reached him, and save for some fears that his late position in the Church might be of dis- I advantage he appears to have given a cheerful acquiesence. Of his success or failure as a teacher we have little evidence. He was devoted to his subject and brought pleasure, possibly enthusiasm, to his work, but his writings reveal a conceit and severity of judgment that do not suggest the patient helper of youth. His best years lay before him though he had already shown himself a scholar of whom Cambridge might well have been proud. He began at Warrington a self-imposed task of preparing a translation of the New Testa- ment with annotations, and the volumes on Matthew's Gospel and the Thessalonian Epistles were printed at Eyres Press. He was a brilliant and widely read,though somewhat slovenly, scholar, and a series of books upon classical subjects are his best contribution to literature. Upon one of these, his "Lucretius," his reputation chiefly rests and his work will probably live as long as the subject has interest for English readers. Some of his views impress us by their oddity. He resigned his classical Tutorship

at Hackney giving as a reason that he objected to public worship, and he defended his singular opinion in "An enquiry into the expediency and propriety of Public and social worship published in 1791. Yet he was a lover of his fellows and humanitarian in the highest degree. In his attitude towards the Slave Trade he was with Wilberforce, and in his views upon war was almost at one with the Quakers. His chief weakness lay in his explosive temper and love of controversy. He was swayed by impulse and scarcely capable of giving mature thought to any subject. Henry Crabb Robinson said of him that "He had the pale complexion and mild features of a saint, was a most gentle creature in domestic life, ancba very amiable man ; but when he took part in political or religious controversy his pen was dipped in gall." IMPRISONED FOR SEDITION. _

With an impetuous -temper and bolding many advanced opinions it was not to be expected that his course would be a smooth one, but few could have predicted the extra- ordinary chapter in his career that tells of a prosecution for seditious libel and :an , imprisonment of two years in Dorchester 1 goal.

_ It was a pamphlet against the Bishop of Llandaff that drew the wrath of his enemies upon him, and though he had the strong support of Charles James Fox and Lord Holland the 'heavy punishment fell. Fox regarded him as a martyr to the cause of liberty and wrote to him on his conviction: "The liberty of the Press I consider as virtually destroyed by the proceedings against Johnson and Jordan and ,what has happened to you 1 cannot but lament there- fore the more, as the sufferings of a man whom I esteem in a cause that is no more." Wakefield had given all he possessed to

compensate his printer and publisher of the pamphlet for their losses in the case, and upon his release from prison a sum of £5,000 was subscribed to put him beyond the reach of want.

===== 0002601_19121109_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. GILBER' BE

rested upon willing shoulders, and especially upon the genial scholar whose voice must be heard each Sunday in the Chapel. Of his previous career a few lines will be sufficient to tell. DR. WILLIAM ENFIELD _

William —infield was the child of poor ' parents who lived at Sudbury in Suffolk, and he was born there March 29th, 1741. On the advice of his tutor, a dissenting minister, he was prepared for the ministry. He had the advantage of five years training at Daventry, and at the age of 22 became minister of the Nonconformist congregation meeting at Benn's Gardens, Liverpool. Nearly seven years had been spent there in a useful ministry, when the invitation came to him which made him at the age of 29 tutor in Belles-Lettres and Rector of the Academy at Warrington. His years of learning had been well spent, and his reputation was well earned. On March Bth, 1774, he had the happiness to receive from the University of Edinburgh the degree of Doctor of Literature.

===== 0002601_19121109_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
A POPULAR WARRINGTON BOOK.

The same year saw the advent of his book "The Speaker," a manual of elocution which ran through countless editions, and held the field until comparatively recent years. The first edition of this famous book was printed at Eyres Press, and is a fine example of the printer's art. Every account of Dr. Enfield shows him to have been an amiable and diligent man, eloquent and persuasive in the pulpit and

===== 0002601_19121109_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HI

delightful in society. Yet he failed in the qualities that the Academy needed. Truth to tell, it was mastership not scholarship that was the need of the hour. The mathematical chair had been filled from 1757 to 1772 by Mr. Holt and upon his death in this latter year Mr. George Walker succeeded him. But the remuneration was too small for his necessities and his term of service scarcely extended to two years. It was upon his resignation in 1774 that Dr. Enfield cheerily stepped into the vacant post.

===== 0002601_19121109_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEATH OF DR. AIKIN.

In 1778 after twenty years of service in the Academy, the last four the most arduous of all, Dr. Aikin began to fail in health. Anl assistant was obtained (an old pupil Mr. P. Houghton), but the doctor's days of service were over. A few months of frail health and he was laid to rest in the Churchyard. Dr. Aikin's life belongs to the life of Warrington and merits more space that can be given here. With admiration none too extravagant Dr Gilbert Wakefield wrote as the text of his memorial tablet:

MS. Reverendi viri Joannis Atkin, D.D. In Academia Warringtoniana Prima Literarum Humaniortun Deinde Theologiae Per Annos Viginti tree Professoris Judicio imptimis lirnato, candito, subtili Liberatium penitiorumque doctrinatum disciplina Instractissimi Comis Benevolus Fitts Et Hominis et Christiani numera Cumulatissime explevit Obiit die quarto decenio Decembris Anno Salutis 1780 /17, tatis 67 Two tutors were now elected as colleagues to Dr. Enfield and they were to remain with him until the Academy was dissolved in 1786,

===== 0002601_19121109_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REAL WEAKNESS OF THE ACADEMY.

This university scholar was welcomed by the Trustees in the hope that his influence would restore the declining favour of the Academy. But intellectual brilliance was not the outstanding need. The Academy had failed to grip the students. Too young and ill-educated, perhaps, at their reception, no one, since Mr. Seddon's day, had been able to use a restraining hand and they had lived in semi-lawlessness.

Their wild pranks are r: ow a subject for mirth, but they formed a solemn and insoluble problem for the distracted rector and tutors who had to witness the Academy drawn thereby to disfavour and ruin. The subjects of .the students must now engage us and we need not perhaps in our sympathy for the Tutors omit all mention of riots and gaieties and regrettable occur- rences, even though those extravagances did spread terror in town and help to break the hearts of good and learned men. This had better be placed in a special chapter. A.M.

THE BUILDING SCHEME.

At a meeting of the Building Committee the Clerk was instructed to write the Local Government Board with reference to the provision of accommodation for epileptics, inquiring whether that Board, in view of its inspector's report, would be prepared to take full responsibility if the scheme suggested by its °fillers were proceeded with, and to deal with all points that the Lunacy Commissioners might raise. The Local Government Board replied that the approval of buildings to be erected at the Workhouse was a matter for that Board. Any questions as to the accommodation of the lunatics, raised by the Lunacy Commissioners in their report after a visit to the Work- house, could be dealt with as they arose. It was decided that Mr Seger Owen be instructed to prepare alternative sketch plans, approximately on the lines suggested by the officers of the Local Government Board, and that these be submitted to the committee, along with the plans of the original scheme, fo consideration. . . _ . . .

Mr JOLLEY, in moving the adoption of the minutee, raid it would be inexpedient to embark upon a dis- cussion of the question,seeing that it had been delayed. It would shortly come before the Board in a more definite form, but at present the whole thing was open. Mr FuRNEss seconded.

The Rev A M MITCIIILLL said he was opposed to any- thing in the nature of heavy expenditure. For one thing it was very doubtful what was going to happen— they did not know exactly where they were—and another reason was that the time of that Board was getting short and it would be as well to leave such a big undertaking to be decided by their successors. There was no need to go on with alternative schemes. The Local Government Board had suggested that new offices should he built in the town. They had con- sidered the question of Rural District Council cffices before and had decided not to proceed with the work. Personally he agreed that if new offices wore to be provided, there ought to be centralization and the offices of the Town Council, the Rural District Council and ' the Board of Guardians placed in one block of build- ings. The CITAIRMAN: I don't think you can do that very well. At the earns time, I think modified schemes should be considered by the committee and sketch plans are accordingly being prepared. You will then have the opportunity of raising the question again. The Rev A M 111rroffELL: I am quite in favour of some kind of temporary building being erected. That is a different thing. The minutes were appproved.

SITPERANIYA.TION ALL-0 WANCE.

The CLERK submit:i-E-d-a—r-e-tnin showing that the number of successful primary vaccinations carried out by the medical officers of the Union, at the cost of the rates, during the year ending September 30th was 18i2. The successful re-vaccinations numbered six. In reply to the Chairman the CLERK said he believed the figures were lower than for the previous year, owing to the number of exemptions granted. Mr FURNESS : I note that the returns refer only to successful vaccinations. Are there no returns of unsuccessful ones? _ _

The CLERK: : They only ray for successful vaccina- tions—that is the reason.

THE OLD QUESTION. When the Clerk P.ubmit,ted a report as to the con- sumption of water on the Workhouse premises, the Rev A M Itittrcitm.t. interjected : You seem to be very fond of water. Can we have a similar statement as to the beer and other things consumed ? WAS IT rotamcm. ?

„ The Thetford Guardians wrote asking the Board to endorse a resolution protesting against the ever- increasing expenditure of County Councils to meet the requirements of the Board of Education being a drain upon local rates, and expressing the opinion that the time bad now arrived when the cost of education, as well as of maintaining main roads, should be national and not local charges. It was requested that copies of the resolution should be forwarded to the Prime Minister, the Chancellor of the Exchequer, and the local M P.'s. .

The CLERK said the resolution referred more to Rural District Councils than to Boards of Guardians. However the Guardians were a public authority, and a resolution pissed by them would lend weight. Their usual practice, however, had been to refer such ques- tions to the Association of Poor Law Unions which represented nearly every Board in England and Wale. Miss BROADBENT moved that the question he referred to the Association of Poor Law Unions.

Mr Backhouse asked if it would not carry more weight if individual Boards passed the resolution and forwarded it to the quarters suggested ? The CHAIRMAN expressed the view that local expres- sions of opinion would be of value. Mr BACKHOUSE moved that the resolution be sent to the Prime Minister, the Chancellor of the Exchequer, the local members of Parliament and the Association of Poor Law Unions. The Rev A M Mum= said that when questions were referred to a central authority in London, they often heard nothing more about them. They were simply shelved. He believed in individuality of action. Mr FURNESS said the resolution was trenching on political matters. Apart from that, he was afraid that if the change suggested were made it would deprive them of local control of education and highways. Once a question got into the hands of Government depart- ments, they could not do anything without their con- sent and knowledge, and red tapeism was the result. An enormous number of extra officials wonld also be created. He moved that the consideration of the resolution be deferred until the next meeting. Mr JOLLEY said the . subject was one of very great importance. It was dealt with by a Royal Commission ten years ago, but nothing had happened. Personally he felt ra her suspicious of anything that seemed to trench on matters which came within the range of ordinary party politics, and the Board had always been chary at dealing with such subjects. Nothing would be lost by delaying the discussion for a fortnight. Ile was afraid that, as a matter of practice, nothing would happen if they did pass the:resolutionz because Govern- ments were hardened to that sort of thing. Conservative and Liberal administrations had been bombarded with such resolutions for years. The Rev A M MITCHELL: I don't understand where the politics oome in. The CHAIRMAN : I don't fee that it is a political question at all. Continuing, he said he did not agree with Mr Furness that the suggested change would take administration out of the hands of the local authorities. If there was a re-arrangement, they would continue to have some voice in the matter. Mr JOLLEY : No pay, no control. The CILAIRMAN said that in regard to the administra- tion of education, they need not be afraid about the future. Hia experience was that local bodies had very little authority even now. It was decided to write the Thetford Board suggest- ing that the matter was one which should be referred to the Association of Poor Law Unions and the Rural District Councils Association.

The Miracle of the Poor Clergy.

In his November letter to his parishioners, the Rev. Walter Greswell, of Marthall, Cheshire, says that in a previous letter in whioh he referred to the gloom of the clergy he did not mean to imply that all his fellow- clergy were gloomy. He says that the clergy are the finest body of men in the world really—at least the poor clergy are. They have every cause to be gloomy, but they are the pluckiest. Do you ever think," he con- tinues, " what it means to be a clergyman in a thousand cases and to hold a living so-called which brings you in 265. a week, with a house to keep up and 'position' to maintain? Can you imagine what it must be like when a clergyman has to go into church Sunday after Sunday and preach a gospel of gladness, and feel all the while that his wife is sinking for lack of food, or the delicate child must die of want, when the butcher and the baker and the grocer attend church, not to hear the sermon Bo much as to remind him that that lone-sttnding account whioh has been hanging like a millstone round his neert is still unpaid ?" "There are 1,491 of these men, who, for all the diffi- cult work they try to do, are rewarded with £67 a year, and this in the most religious and the most wealthy country in the world. Congress meets, but never a word is breathed about these men. Congress discusses miracles, but the greatest miracle of all is not referred to. The greatest miracle of sll is not that the churches are so empty of worshippers but that our gaols are not full of clergy. "
