TAXATION OF LAND VALUES IN CANADA. • MR. F. NEILSON, ALP., AND THE FREEST CITY

IN THE WORLD.

Mr Francis Neilson, M.P., lectured at Compstall, Marple. on Saturday on his recent tour through the United States and Canada. He said that Edmonton was the freest town or city on the face of the earth. There was no private owner drawing monopoly valurs out of the people of Edmonton. The people enjoyed their own monopoly value. They levied their taxes for local purposes upon land values. There was no tax on improvements; there was no tax on personal property, no business tax, no income tax, no death duties tax, no indirect tax with the exception of the Imperial protective tax, and that they would shift immediately if they had their own way. They taxed land values everywhere, and the greatest brains in the State and Canada were full of the gospel of Henry George. We ought to be able to shift things very soon in this ocuntrv. They did nt want to tax the land. They wanted to tax the value of the land, which was quite another thing. They wanted to untax the farms and levy the taxes underne ith the farms. We must get to work. They had presented a manifeso to the Prime Minister and the Chancellor of the Exchequer in which they demanded that all land values should be available for public needs. lie was going to take good care that the Government did something with the land when they had got the v duation completed, otherwise he would not be true to his pledges. They must level a flat tax without exemption. All, rich and poor, must be in the scheme for justice.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Tho Disadvantage of Riches.—Whiht the rovement in the direction of starting schools of domestic economy is extending to all grades of society, Lady Aberconway thias that women of the wealthy classes should be taught an occupation, even when marriage is considered a likely destiny. There are many women with ,Aealthy family surroundings who feel that their lives Ire more or less spoiled, because riches prevent them fro -gt find- ing an outlet for their talents. They grow weary of being purely ornamental appendages, in the eternal round of society amusements, and become envious of the fuller lives of the less well-to-do. There are only two ways Lady Aberconway says, for a woman of the upper classes to obtain money—by inheritance from the dead, or begging it from the living, and in both respects neither the law of the land, or the custom of society, favour women. If therefore there is a lot of wasted talent lying idle, why not use it? There is no reason at all, only as Lady Aberconway admits "the obtaining of money from the living has been found to be an affair of some difficulty, and much skill." That is what the would-be workers in the upper ranks of society generally fail to realise. There are long years of infinite drudgery to go through before one can hope for a leading appoin- ment as nurse, or in any professional capacity, hut there are, of course, always hobbies on which the workless rich can exercise their abilities. The Civil Service Typists.—The Civil Service Typists' Association has a social as well as a pro- fessional objective, and the Hobbies Exhibition, organised at Caxton Hall, provided an interesting display, as sNowing the favourite relaxations of girl typists. Since its foundation in 1903, the association has assisted in bringing about a marked improvement in regard to the pay and status of Civil Service Typists, which must tend to Improve the position of typists generally. In 1904, as the result of a petition to the Treasury, the minimum salary of typists was raised from Ms to lfis a week. Subsequently the minimum was increased to 20s, rising by 2s a year to Ns, and the ' typists have since secured eligibility fr promotion to higher grades. The association seeks to raise the typists to the status of women clerks, and to secure that generally denied measure of justice in the com- mercial and industrial world, namely to obtain an equal salary for men and women employed with the same' results on the same class of work. _ _

Longer Lived Women.—Every year, more and more trades and occuration fined places for women. This invasion of men's sphere of work has been going on for a quarter of a century or more, but, although it has involved a general change from more sheltered con- ditions of home life, hard work and rougher times do not appear to have adversely affected the health of womankind, or their hold on life. Government life annuitants are, no doubt, a special class, and statistics relating to them, may not be applicable to the popula- tion as a whole, but what is significant in the figures issued by the department is, that the expectation of life of a woman of fifty is now more than a year greater than it was in 1875, while that of a man of the same age is only about three months greater. Women, it would seem, are flourishing, on the more strenuous and exposed conditions of life, but it would also appear that some of the advantage, partly due to economic conditions, is at the expense of the male element seeing that the gain in length of life is so much more marked in women than in men. The Toy-making Industry.—Some of the fancy shops are already preparing for Christmas, and the present- giving craze is already beginning to assert itslf. There is no lack of ingenuity in this year's fancy output, and searchers will have no difficulty in finding novelties in the way of little gifts suitable for working for their friends. Such presents, which introduce the f personal element, are always more appreciated than purchases at Christmas bazaars. These shopmg attractions promise a bigger display than ever and it is satisfactory to learn that British enterprise and British skill, are supplying the market to an increasing extent. At last our manu- facturers seem to have wakened up to the importance of the toy and fancy goods industry, which is no longer a German commercial monopoly. The making of dolls, and of wooden and stuffed toys Is being extensively carried on in Buckinghamshire and other counties, and it is only in the making of doll faces, that our home manufacturers cannot beat the Germans, who are very clever in safeguarding trade secrets. Toy and doll making are largely carried on by woman and girls, in the workers' own homes, and, if paid for at satisfactory rates,it provides useful employ-

ment in rural districts. There would be legs to be said in its favour if it falls into the category of sweated industries, which, despite twentieth century progress still reduce to conditions, little better than slavery, ten of thousands of struggling female workers. The Mixing of Colours.—There is quite a craze for wearing the most incongruous colours at one and the same time, and the results in tho majority of cases are I disastrous. Very few people have the sense of colour fully developed, and to employ live or six colours successfully in one toilette, would tax the powers of a trained and skilled artist. Yet persons who are utterly devoid of the sense ofcolour, cheerfully choose shades that kill each other, and utterly destroy their own colouring and then see nothing amiss. Purple is a beautiful colour, that suits blondes and brunettes, and fortunately this hue is very fashionable just now. It makes a wholesome change from blue and grey, in which so many suits are built. There are divers shades some of which are suited to the elderly, others to the youthful, for purple becomes youth and age alike. , The Accordion-Heated Blouse.—Among the newest of this season's popular blouses, is the charming little garment of accordion-pleated ninon, chiffon, crepe-de- chine, or any transparent material, which is worn over a coloured soft silk slip. These blouses have short basques also accordion-pleated, and are finished with a belt at the waist of tome richly coloured brocade. These belts have lone short end, finished with a thick fringe, such as cheuille or heavy silk. Many girls embroider sashes and belts to wear with different dresses in coloured silks, or beads and tangles. These are always made with wide pleats at the waist, the embroidered, jetted or beaded ends being not more than twelve inches in length. Some very charming evening dresses for quite young girls are of accordion pleated:ninon over a pretty coloured silk. These dresses are simplicity itself, and are made without sashes or weistbelts, a narrow gaug- ing of ninon with a lace edging on either side defining the waist which may be high, or normal. This gauging is frequently used for the accordion pleated blouse, but the sash is naturally more ornate and gives a smarter appearance and finish to the whole. llsed Grasses.—Some of the graceful hedgerow grasses, so plentiful just now, afford charming decora- tions throughout the winter, if carefully dryed and dyed. First and foremost they should be hung up in a stymy window for a few days, then the dying process must be commenced. For a good re4l brown, which shows up well with white chrysanthemums, a little per- mauganate of potassium dissolved in water is excellent, while for a clear orange tint eome red ink should be put into a vessel, and to every tablespoonful a pinch of saffron should be added. For a lovely purple tint one part of copying ink to six of water is best. Quaking grass died with red ink makes a most effective addition to vases or bowls of white flowers and red berries.

LORD ROBERTS AND RMANY. Protest By Manchester Germans.

On the occasion of a dinner of the German " Vorein " in Manchester on Saturday, Mr. F. Zimmern, who pres- sided, said they would no doubt have road with regret the speech delivered the other thy by Lvd Roberts. They know very well that, though he was deservedly honoured as a distinguished soldier, he was altogether mistaken as to the policy of the Fatherland —a policy of peace and peaceful develop- ment. The wars of ISGG and 1870, to which Lord Roberts referred, were necessary for the consolidation of German unity, end in defence of her very existence. History had given its verdict. Ile asked them to join him in condemning such a speech as harmful to the good understanding between the two nations, which is so much to be desired, and he begged all of them to assure their friends in Germany that Lord Roberts's words did not reflect the opinion of the great majority of Ecgliah poople. "They hold," Mr Zimmern said, "the sa_ae view 1,23 we do, and we have seen it by the loud protests which have been made by influential people on every side." Mr. Zimmern's remarks were received with applause.

WOMEN LIBERALS.

MR. AND MRS. A. H. CROSFIELD AT A WiRD SOCIAL.

TORY UNEASINESS AND GLOOM.

The Warrington Women's Liberal Association held the first of a series of ward sccials on Monday even- ing when members and friends in St. Austin's Ward met in the Thewlis-street schoolroom. The affair was a splendid success and augurs well for the winter session's work of this organization which is one of the most active and flourishing branches of the progressive forces. Over 200 sat down to tea and at at the subsequent concert the room was filled to its utmost capacity. Mr. and Mrs. A. 11. Crosfield attended and had an exceedingly cordial reception. The arrange- ments for the gathering had been admirably carried out by the ward captains—Mrs. E. V. Walker and Miss Booth, who had willing assistance girom members of the general committee and from the indefatigable hon. secretary (Mrs Edmund Osborne). Mrs E A Sanders had charge of the musical pro- gramme mhich was well-varied and highly enjoy- able. Miss Crosfield delighted the audience with a couple of songs, the flute obligato to one of them being supplied by Miss Le Thick°, while Mrs. Crosfield played the pianoforte accompaniments. Songs were effectively given by Mrs Sanders, Mrs Sephton and Miss Hewitt, and duets by Mr and Mrs Sephton. Mr Alf Dutton was very successful in his recitations and Mr S Aukland was es usual, a great favourite in his clever sketch and concertina solos. Mr T F Walling- ton was an efficient accompanist. Mrs A H CROSFIELD, in the course of a teat end effective speech, said they were holding their first social under very auspicious circum- stances. They wera all feeling happy and elated at their successes in the municipal electiors which bad been very gratifying to the Liberal Party. (Applause.) They congratulated the Liberal Councillors who had been successful in their elections and they also congratulated Councillor Osborne— (applause)—on the splendid victory he had achieved in that ward. Of course that was greatly due to his own energy and to the esteem entertained for him, but she must add that it was not surprising that he should have won, with a better half like Mrs Osbcsme. (Loud applause.) They were all proud of her and knew hew greatly her energy and initiative had contributed to her husband's triumph. (Applause.) The results of the electiors augured well for the spirit of Lioeraiism in Warrington. She heard rumours about the

UNEASINESS THAT SEEMED TO BE SPREADING through the Tory ranks. (Laughter.) s They wore especially anxious when mention was made of a certain building that was shortly to be erected. (Applause). She did not know whether all thee() present bad heard about it, but she hoped and felt certain that there would be a special room in the enlarged club for the Women's Liberal Association. (Arplause). The now building would put the Conservative Club in the shade. (Laughter). The plight of the Tory party reminded her of the fate of the Turkish Army in the struggle in the Balkans. (Applause). She was very specially interested in that conflict and she thought it was scandalous that in England, which had always stood for freedom and justice, there were still some people who were uphold- ing the Turks and hoping for their success. (" Shame ") There should not be a single English man or woman to back up the Ottoman forces and the only excuse to be found for those who did so was ignorance. They must not know the conditions which obtained or realize the

===== 0002601_19121109_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
_BARBARITY AND CRUELTY

of which the Turks were capable. The Turks were not civilized ; they were absolute barbarians. They were about COO years behind the times ; they were not Christians, and they thought all Christians were dogs. They had been promised that the more Christians they killed, the better chance they would have of going to heaven. That was their morel code' so it was easy to imagine their conduct in warfare. Before fleeing from villages they killed all the women and children and subjected the men to horrible tortures. She did not think there was any possibility that the Turks would in this war—it was practically all over with them. (Applause.) It was difficult to conceive how the Powers had tolerated Turkish misrule and oppression so long. Now that the poor little Balkan States had taken the situation into their bands and had dectded to liberate themselves, the Powers were beginning to talk about the distribution of the territory which the Balkan States hal been winning at the cost of so many lives. What right had they to talk about doing that ? It was the Balkan States who must dis- tribute their own country—(applause)—and she did not think they would stand interference. Referring to a threatened " war " nearer home, Mrs Crosfield alluded to the conduct of Sir Edward Carson and other leaders who had been attempting to stir up rebellion in Ulster. It was hoped that now he had seen the horrors and suffering which war caused, Sir Edward would refrain from talking about civil war in Ireland. She was afraid, however, that many people were disinclined to take Sir Edward Carson and his allies very seriously. They knew that empty vessels always made the most sound. (Laughter.) The Insurance Act which was now in operation would be of untold benefit to millions of men and women, but a small section of the people—the doctors—were making a great deal of fuss about it. Mr Lloyd George had met them most generously and if they did not accept his offer she was afraid they would regret it. (Applause). Mr AII CROSFIELD, who bad a most enthusiastic reception,said that among all the meetings he attended, quite the most cheerful were those of the Women's Liberal Association. That evening they had special reason to be in a happy frame of mind, and probably the most cheerful person in whole room was his good friend Mrs Osborne. (Applause.) It was largely owing to her splendid work in co-operation with her husband, that the victory in St Austin's Ward was won—and it was not the only fine success they as progressives could rejoice in. (Applause.) Ho did not believe it was the Liberals who originally brought party politics into those municipal contests, but that might be a disputed niktter. At any rate, they had to accept the situation and a right good account they gave of themselves last week. (Applause.) An additional cause for satisfaction was to be found in the work that was going on in the party as a whole. (Hear, hear.) John Bright defined human happiness as: A pleasurable occupation with a sense of progress," and it must give all Liberals pleasure to see the good, practical, positive work for which their party was respmsible. In the old days no end of time, ability, and first•class capacity was wasted through the obstruc- tive policy of the House of Lords, but now they had the knowledge that their efforts would not be thrown away. (Applause.) When they heard talk about the Liberal party coming a cropper over the Insurance Act, they could only regard it as one more example of the short-sightedness of their political opponents. (Applause.) When the measure was intro- duced they welcomed it as a fine constructive measure of social reform which would help to build up and regenerate the nation. They sang its praises and he (the speaker) thought they were going to have, for once,7an instance of co-operation in carrying out a good work. Speaking impartially, it struck him as both . AMAZING AND DEPLORAIMAII .

that the Tory party could suddenly turn "right about face" and make a dead set againt the Act, just because they saw a chance of working up unpopularity and securing a few votes during the few months when it was a question of paying premiums, and waiting for benefits to come into operation. It was a striking evidence of the way in which tho Tory party had lost caste of late years. It was said that the Act wanted amending, but they could not learn in any better way how it could be improved upon than by watching it in actual operation. They must not lose their sense of proportion. The Act as it stood was

A MASTERPIECE OF STATESMANSHIP. (Applause.) In London there were experts and per- manent officials—not hot-headed partizans, but men of cool,critical intellect—who were examining the Act and he knew the opinion of some of them. They said how foolish the Tory party was to think that Mr Lloyd George was merely a man with oratorical gifts on the platform. One of the very ablest of those experts declared the other day: "The more I look into that Act the more I am struck with tho foresight, the comprehensiveness of view, and the true statemanship that has been put into it." (Applause). The Govern- ment had made the long-promised and much-needed provision for the old people—(applause)—and at the same time Mr Herbert Samuel carried through a most admirable Bill to protect the children. Old age pensions had, to use the Words of Mr Birroll, brought more sunshine into humble homes than anything that had been done for many years. (Applause.) Now they had provision for the millions of people upon whom sickness and adversity fell end for those saddest of c%aases o s sof to prematurebegiven andto the permanent mothers ob fr Idg ol awnnd. a t Help the was when it was of most value. People of all parties had been talking about the curse of the slums in England. But Mr Lloyd George was not content to bear these evils talked about and to wait tens of years while musty reports were accumulated and drowsy Commissions sat. He went straight to the root of the matter— (applause) —and in future it would not pay slum owners to neglect their duty. Consequently life in the congested districts of our towns and cities would be purified and made little morecivilized,in the true sense of the word. (Applause.) In addition, Mr Lloyd George fiad taken far-reaching and most comprehensive measures for dealing on a large scale with the greatest scourge of our land. The Government was embarking on a

CONSTANTINOPLE.

The succession of successes of the Bulgarian army during the last week removes many obstacles to a Bulgarian march upon Constantinople. The imminent fall of Adrianople will set free a large number of siege guns and enable the whole Bulgarian army to concentrate on the Tchatalja fortifications before Constantinople. Our photograph of Constantinople, taken from the Golden Horn, shows the great Mosque of St. Sophia, believed to be the finest example of the Byzantine style, the first stone of which was laid in 532, on the site of several successive churches of the same name.

CRUSADE AGAINST CONSUMPTION

and in a shO;i, time- anatoria would .1-De—s-p-rin-ging up all over England, where sufferers could be sent before it was too late and get the quiet, rest, and pure sweet air of the country. How many thousands of cases there would be where fatal breakdown would be avoided and heart-broken people raved from wrecked homes ! The two great measures of social reform which he bad referred to were typical of the work that was being done by the G,vernment. Oa a smaller scale be would like to mention—because women were immediately concerned in it—the measure which for the first time in England had practically introduced the rruscrrum OF THE MINIMUM WA GE. _ .

===== 0002601_19121109_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
= (Applause.) Only a few trades, in which sweating had been going on, had been selected, quite impartially, but the principle thus established would be gradually extended. Above a certain minimum, competition would still be loft to regulate things, but it must not be allowed to drive wages below that strata. The State had to step in and protect those who were not in a position to protect themselves. (Applause) The measure was passed after consultation with the best expert advice that could he obtained from men and women, and he felt a deep respect for the women who, week in and week out, were engaged in that splendid kind of social reform. (Applause.) One of those who had taken the lend in connection with the Trades Board Act—Miss Constant Smith—(applause)—was present at the recent conference in connection with the Inter- national Association for Labour Legislation and he had seldom listened to anything more impressive, sincere or instructive than what Mrs Smith said in a very simple speech about the work she had done among the least fortunate people whose interests they were striving to defend. In concluding she said that while problems between different nationalities were serious enough, in all conscience, she thought that the diffi- culties of statesmen might be lessened and their co-operation made a little easier, if they realized how leading men and women in different countries could unite in efforts to make a little less hard the lot of suffering humanity. (Applause.) These were days' concluded Mr Crostield, of groit hope for the Liberal party. From what he had seen and read in Warrington during the last few days, their friends on the other side seemed rather blue just now. (Laughter.) Certainly they were a little GLOOMY AT THE CONSERVATIVE CLUB the other night—(laughter)—although he noticed that his friend Mr Fulton Smith was doing a little whistling to keep his spirits up. (Laughter.) Their Tory friends might be excused for feeling a little down, under the circumstances, because, as the "Examiner " said, it was just possible they were thinking that coming events were casting their shadows before. (Laughter and applause). The Rev. W. COOPER delivered a short speech, in which he emphasised the part which the women of the ' ward and of the town must play in local politics if they wished to see Mr. Crosfirld once more at the bead of the poll. They were going on the right lines in St. ' Austin's, and he hoped the progress would be main- tained. The rev, gentleman counselled his hearers to beware of the twin evils of present-day Conservatism— Tariff Reform and Conscriptirn. One would make the rich man richer at the expense of poorer people, and the other would increase the power in the hands of the wealthy and enable them to still further oppress the working-classes. He said democracy must rule, and in Mr. Crosfleld they had a true friend of the workers— and one who, though not now in the House of Commons, was working earnestly through the international Labour Association (of which he was a prominent member) to better the conditions of employment in this country and all over the world. (Applause). Mrs. CROSFISiD presented the prize awarded in tbe bread-making ocmpetition to Mrs. Unsworth,l3,Samuel- street. A vote of thanks to Mrs. E. V. Walker and Mies Booth, the captains of the ward, and to Mrs. Sanders for her share in arranging the concert party, brought a most successful gathering to a close.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT . COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council was held in Bewsey Chambers on Wednesday afternoon, Mr G Mather presiding. There were also present :--Rev A M Mitchell, Dr Murray, Messrs W Furness, J Backhouse, W Houghton, A Brooks, J Ball, J W Knowles, P Longton, J C Sutton (clerk), B S Newcombe (surveyor), II II Burnage (assistant surveyor), F Kerfoot (inspector), Dr Sephton (medical officer).

CLOSING OF FOOTPATH. At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee on October 30th the Clerk reported the receipt of a letter from the Clerk of the Penketh Parish Council setting forth correspondence re the closing of a footpath at Fiddler's Ferry and asking that the District Council would take the necessary action to protect the interests of the public. After some discussion by the committee the consideration of the question was deferred. The Surveyor was instructed to visit the spot and report to the Council.

===== 0002601_19121109_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
SANATORIA BENEFIT.

At a meeting of-ih;tie'nOr7al'ie-ri:o-s-es Committee the Clerk reported the receiving ofan official communication from the Clerk of the County Council re the division of the County administrative area into tuberculosis areas and enclosing a plan showing a division into ten areas. The Clerk had submitted the suggested plan to Dr SeFhton and read a letter from him. After examin- ing the plan the committee considered tho proposed arr ingements of dispensary accommodation to be unsatisfactory, and they advised that the County Council be asked to provide a branch dispensary within their district. It was decided that the Clerk write to the County Council stating that the Rural Council think it neces- sary to have a branch dispensary within their district. FADGATE SEWAGE QUESTION. The Surveyor Mr B S Newcombe had prepared a sewage Echeme for the district on the gravitation system' but the consulting engineer ' considered it would be necessary to lift the sewage before treatment as the gravitation system was always open to flooding from the river, lie also con- sidered it would be necessary to provide a scheme for four times the population and for the whole township. lie estimated that what would be necessary to do for some time would cost £8,936 including treatment,which almost coincided with the estimate for the surveyors scheme. MEDICAL OFFICERS REPORT. The Medical Officer's report, which was adopted, stated that during the month there had been 28 births and 26 deaths. The death rate from all causes was equal to 22.3 per 1,000 as against 317 average for the last three mouths and 21 for the corresponding month rat year;

ANOTHER CUSTOM HOUSE STORY. The Queensland sugar refiner's experience referred to in last week's " Notes and Notions" reminded me of an incident in the career of a young Dutchman of my acquaintance some years ago. A native of Hengelo, Holland, he came to Lancashire to learn a certain trade, and as he lodged near my place and went my way in a morning, we often walked along [together. During the holidays he went home for a spell, and he promised to bring back a quantity of cigars for his friends. He confided to us that they were really "dirt cheap " where he came from. I ventured to ask if he had not found that the duty brought up the price to pretty much what he would have had to pay in England. He confessed that he had overlooked the fact that there would be that duty to pay,but added that the Customs officer who challenged him happened to be a " decent sort" Ile took him quietly on one side, and whispered—" Look here, you've let yourself in for something heavy on this lot; but give me half a sovereign, and we'll say no more about it this tiu3o !" And with that the foreigner passed along triumphantly with his box of cigars. As I have said, that happened years ago. S,mehow, one would fain bore that nothing of that sort is possible nowadays. SANATORIUM " BENEFIT " (?) As to this, if I had space I should like to review the situation in the light of recent developments, as, under circumstances previously explained, I have had the advantage of personal inspection of a sanatorium for pthysical patients, and there obtained up-to-date information from a specialist, together with significant if not unique admissions by an experienced resident official. 1 am therefore in possession of sufficient material to at least suggest that public authorities charged with these responsibilities should proceed economically by easy experimental stages ; and that prospective patients should not build too confidently on such aids as an unfailing means of restoration to normal health. Tho hopes as to complete and final cure which some had entertained have received a rule shock after experience of sanatorium treatment ; and I commend the caution of those public authorities who are acting tentatively in this regard. It must suffice for the moment to state that evidence I have accumulated goes indisputably to show that, unless a patient has the advantage of sanatorium treatment before the consumption has made much progress, the sufferer can hardly expect permanent benefit from it. That may by some be deemed eelf-evident and even trite. Anyhow, there is more behind it, as I think I could show were I at liberty to go further into detail. However, that is not possible juiat now, and I must pass on. THE DARK CONTINENT. Monday's lecture by Dr.Burford Hooke sent me back upon an old-time experience of my own. When Stanley returned from his historic exploit in Darkest Africa, it was my lot to report his first public lecture on " How I found Livingstone." The enterprising promoters turned out rather a fussy little knot of self-important personages, and they had evidently thought of "sort o' chumming-up" with the famous explorer. When be arrived, they conducted burn to an ante-room of the hall in which he was to speak, and it was plain that they intended to spend the interval in familiar chat. Stanley took in the situation at a glance and unceremoniously cleared the room telling the bewildered busybodies that he would be ready to fulfil his engagement at the appointed hour, and that mean- while that apartment was private. SJine of the pro- moters told me afterwards that, while quite satisfied with the lecture and results, they had found Stanley "awfully stuck-up and staud-offish." VIVISECTION. One has indeed reason to be proud of the Mayor's presidency of the Warrington Anti-Vivisection Society, whose annual meeting took place on Wednesday evening. For myself I agree that the objections to the practice of vivisecticn as carried on in England are simply these :—(1) Vivisection involves cruelty, and cruelty is evil. (2) The animal on which -is imposed the vivisectional experiment is in an abnormal state (one of nuesthesta or of suffering) and hence the experiment is usually worthless. As to the former point, it will be said that am:esthetics are used to prevent pain. In his evidence before the Royal Com- mission, Dr. Rolleston said (inter alia) :—" It is not so easy a thing to know when you have an animal thoroughly ancesthetised The whole ques- tion of anmsthetising animals has an element of uncer- tainty about it." Moreover, I have always under- stood- and I have been interested in the subject for many years— that the majmity of experi- ments are perfcrmed without amesthetics. To

into it." He said that he thanked God for the willing men who volunteered for our army, and endorsed Lord Robert's speech on the question of our country being totally unprepared for war, and said that it was his belief that the only way to get peace was to be ready for war. It was the duty of our youths to be willing to serve their country in this way. "how can we do our duty ?" By consecrating our bodies to God we shall then be ready to carry out our duties to the State. It is only by God's love for us that we are able to be here to-day, and so we must do our duty to Him first. Let it be heart service and not word only. Do not look at Christ as a lovely man only but regard His pierced hands, for he must be a living Man to you. Let us go home this afternoon determined to give ourselves to God. Be interested in the place we live in, and in our country. Elect the right men for our rulers, and never mind politics ; cm sick of party politics. Let each one say to himself that he will be a man who will do his duty. Let your religion be one that will let you put your hand deep into your pocket so as to help the sick and poor. Selfishness is the root of many of our failings. Put God first in your every- day life, in your home and Church life. The question of doing your duty to God will be followed by duty to State and you will become true citizens. PRIZE DAY AT GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

Saturday was " prize day " at the Lymm Grammar School, and there was a large gathering in the after- noon to welcome Professor Weiss, of Manchester University, who distributed the awards and made an interesting comparison between British and Continental secondary schools. The Rev U B Thurston presided, and was supported by Professor Weiss, Mr Cecil H S \Vinson, the headmaster, the Rev E P Wethered, Mr A Watkm,Mr G L Welforo Mr and Mrs Ridgway and Mrs Callender. During the afternoon a number of glees and part songs were excellently rendered by a number of the school pupils. THE HEAD MASTRR'S REPORTS. The report of the Joint Matriculation Board of the Universities of Manchester, Liverpool, Leeds and Sheffield upon the examination, which was read by the Headmaster, stated that the school was undoubtedly doing good work ; the material at its command was not of a very promising nature, but it would appear that the new accommodation, reorganized curriculum and stag, and uninterrupted period of solid work was all that was required to produce good results. It would appear that, under wise guidance, there was much to be said for the system of co-education. In the Lyn= Grammar School the difficulties seemed to ba success- fully met. But it was urged that it is in the highest degree important that children brought up on that plan should he taken very young. In his own report, the Headmaster said that the new school buildings had proved themselves an inestimable boon to teachers and pupils. Commenting upon the change in the system of examination which had been made, he said it was hoped and believed that the altera- tion was one that would prove permanently beneficial to the school. The Northern Universities Joint Board had got in the thin end of the wedge of what !must inevitably become ideal examinations in which there would be co-operation between teachers, scholars, and the examiners. The numbers in the school last term were 131, and now there were 137 pupils, which number reached the high-water mark. There seemed to be every likelihood that the extra accommodation would be more than fully occupied. (Applause). The Chairman congratulated the Headmaster and the staff on the satisfactory state of the school. He added that the extra number of pupils asked for when the increased accommodation was provided, had been more than obtained, and they now felt quite safe with regard to the future and solvency of the school.

PROFESSOR_ WEISS ON SECONDARY SCHOOLS.

Professor Weiss, in the course of an interesting address said he had had experience of secondary schools in Germany, Switzerland and England. One was, there- fore, interested in seeing the variety of types of schools cud methods of education. In Germany and in Switzer- land the inconvenient question of solvency had not weighed upon the secondary schools in the unfortunate manner in which it had pressed upon them in England, where so many sckools in the pat struggled and went under. He hoped in the future that would not be the case. One feature which, coming from continental secondary schools to those at home, struck one very considerably was that while in some respects we might fall behind in one or other method of training, English schools had always stood, not merely for the development of intellect and the training of the powers of observation, but also for the training of character which was a very important field . of influence in the school. It was impossible to imagine how serious would be the out- come if we turned out merely very good scholars who had not learnt something else in school that was of even greater value than scholarship, and that was good citizenship. After all it was well to remember that righteousness exalteth a nation. Intellectual standard was of advantage, but the moral worth of the pupils was of far greater value-to the nation. He was glad to see that things which trained character, individual energy, straightforwardness, and honesty were not neglected at that school. (Applause). _

TILE NEED FOII A. SWIMMING BATH. Mr Ridgway, in proposing a vote of thanks to Pro- fessor Weiss, drew attention to the splendid facilities for recreation which the school possessed. He referred to the fact that the District Council was promoting an Act of Parliament,and said it would gladden the heart of the headmaster if the Council could include in it power to erect a swimming bath. He thought the inhabitants of Lymm would feel grateful to the repre- sentatives on the District Council if that scheme could be included. It would be a pity if the growing popula- tion did not learn to switn.. -

Mr G L Welford seconded the motion. Ile said if the Council got the Act of Parliament he hoped that one of the outcomes of that Act would be that they would be able to provide a good swimming bath, not only for the school children, but the public generally. (Applause).

The Examiner, Saturday, November 9, 1912.

The Runcorn Urban Council had a lengthy debate on Tuesday evening on the feasability of taking the town's supply of water from the Vyrnwy main. Runcorn's water is considered among the hardest in England, and some time ago it was decided to mix it in equal propor- tions with Liverpool Corporation water. Recently, owing to a breakdown of the engine' the unmixed Vyrnwy supply was used for several weeks. Mr Pringle moved that Runcorn take its water supply from Liverpool. He said it would mean Gd in the £ on the rates, but the people demanded it, saying they would save the money in soap alone. The sales of soap and soda in the Runcorn grocers' shops had fallen fifty to sixty per cent. lately. Mr Gibbs said the Council had put electric pumping plant recently at the waterworks which would be rendered useless under the proposal. Mr Littler said the poor property-owner was a reality in Runcorn, and this proposal would increase his burdens ern rmously. Mr Baker said that an analysis of Runcorn's present water showed that three heaped cartloads of salt were sent out to 20,000 inhabitants daily. Runcorn people were taking magnesium salts moroing, noon and night, and he appealed to the doctors of the town as to that effect. At present the population could only get soft water by utilising fermenting and dirty raintubs, which were a great source of danger in the backyards. Mr Pritchard recoiled that £70,000 had been spent in the purchase of Runcorn Waterworks in 1893, and the ratepayers would have to provide £2,700 yearly for yet years et in interest and principal. Mr Taylor said Runcorn was out to get manufac- tuaers, but how could they hope to do so with rates at as plus a tremendous water-rate. Mr Imison said the proposal was a wild cat one, the most mad suggestion over brought before the Council. The Council decided to continue the supply of the mored water.

PENKETH.

-- - FRIENDS ScHoot.,—A lantern lecture entitled "A Thousand Miles over the'great Slave Route" was given with limelight views in the Lecture Hall at the above school by Mr J Burtt.—The Lecturer informed the audience that having been sent out to investigate the conditions ef labour on the cocoa plantations on the West Coasts of Africa by Messrs Cadbury, Fry end Rowntree, he had been able to secure a number of slides on his journey, which were meat interesting.—At the I close of the lecture, the best thanks of the company was given to Mr Burtt, WESLEY l;t1ILD LITERARY SECTION.--011 Thursday evening the committee of the above section were fortunate in arranging a very interesting evening for the Guilders,having secured the services of Mr lledley Taylor, of Warrington, to give a lecture entitled "The incessant warfare of Nature." The meeting was held in the large room at the Wesleyan Sunday School. The Rev H Middlewood, president of the Guild was in the chair and introduced Mr Taylor to the audience. The lecturer had a hearty welcome and in his opening remarks said that he did not intend to dwell on the different pictures put on the screen in detail but would endeavour to say suffi- cient to arouse their interest and endeavour to persuade them to educate themselves by inquiring into the subject more. The pictures consisted chiefly of animal life and the Lecturer touched on the points of interest in connection with the different ways animals were protected and also the power they had to take up the offensive. The Rev J Gunnell proposed the best thanks of the meeting to Mr Taylor, and Mr Appleton seconded. Mr Bates was the lanternist.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

RUNAWAY HUSBAND SENTENCED.

James Littler, of Pool-lane, Runcorn, was sent to gaol for six months, at the sessions on Monday,charged with wife neglect. On September 7th the prisoner left home with a young married woman, making no pro- vision for his own wife, who had an eight-months-old baby. She had no money, and when the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children took action, the baby was discovered to weigh Sibs. instead of the 14lbs. which is the weight of a normal child.

===== 0002601_19121109_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
A CHURCH CHOIR STRIKE AT RUNCORN.

THE AUTHORITY OF THE CHOIRMASTER. Over half the members of the choir of St. Michael's Runcorn, are on strike, and the result was noticeable at the services on Sunday. The choirmaster, who is also organist, expelled a boy for impudent behaviour, and was asked by the clergy to agree to certain rules regarding the admission and dismissal of choristers. He tendered his resignation on the ground ;that these rules took all authority out of his hands. The choir reoently held a meeting and urged that the organist should be asked to withdraw his resignation. As this course has not been followed, 18 men and boys have "struck." NEW POST FOR WIDNES OFFICIAL..

Dr 6—g.lire—b—ner,- the .imedical officer of health for Widnes, was on Thursday evening appointed medical officer of health to the Chiswick Urban District Council. There were thirty-three candidates for the post, eleven of whom were interviewed by the Council yesterday. The salary attaching to the office is £.SCO a year, and the successful candidate will, in addition to carrying out the duties of medical officer of health,have the posts of medical attendant at the Isolation Hospital and school medical officer assigned to him.l

WE USE NO DROPS OR DRUGS

THE RELIEF OF 'WIDOWS.

Mr Davies submitted the report of the relief super- vision Committee on the question of outdoor relief for widows and children. The report stated that the Com- mittee considered there was no necessity to alter the standing orders in regard to Widows and that each case be brought to the notice of the Committee by the relieving officers and considered on its merits. He moved the confirmation of the report for the reason that they had adopted the principle of granting more than Is Gd in necessitous cases. There were however, cases and cases. There were some cases where relief would not be granted of more than Is 6d per child, but in hard cases the relief supervision committee had, not for the first term, granted more than Is Gd per per child, and the Committee thought there was no necessity to change the standing orders, seeing that it mentioned at the commencement of the regulations " except in special cases." They relied to a great extent on the relieving officers and the reports they brought. They had every confidence in them and when they recommended more than Is 6d they knew there was good ground for doing so. (Hear, hear.) Mr Hallows (the original mover of the resolution) sscondrd and said be was quite satisfied with the arrangen ants made. Mr Mortis, who was at the Committee meeting for a short time said it was only a question of the relieving officers realising the amount of latitude placed in their hands by the year book. Up to the present they had not realised that they bad however to extend relief and they had kept it down in most cases to is. 6d. per child. . .

Mr Woodward said that the special conditions should he raantioned at the time the increased relief was asked Zor. Thera should be some explanation given when the standing order was departed from. The report was then adopted. ADDITIONAL IZELIE F.

The Guardians of the City of London Union wrote stating that they were prepared to sanction the same additional relief being given during the winter to their non-resident poor in their Parish, as the Runcorn Union might decide to grant to its own poor in receipt of outdoor relief. An additional sum of 6d per week had been granted from the week ending December 7th, until March 29th next. The Clerk said he thought all cases ought to be treated alike. If they had a non-resident case they ought not to treat it differently from their own cases. The Clerk was instracted to reply to the effect I hat this Board did not increase relief for any particular portion of the year.

THE LATE MRS. W. J. FORSHAW. Funeral at Hill Cliffe.

The affection and respect in which the late Mrs W J Forshaw, of Ivy Mount. Latchford, was held by all who knew her, found striking demonstration in the , large and representative attendance at the funeral which took place in Hill Cliffe burial ground on Saturday afternoon. Leaving Ivy Mount at 1.45 the cortege pro- ceeded to Holy Trinity Church, with which the deceased lady 'had teen closely identified for many years. Here the first part of the burial service was impressively conducted by the Rev E C E Carleton (vicar of Holy Trinity Church) and the Rev G C Briggs vier of Christ Church, Latchford). At the close the (organist (Mr W B Randall) played "0 rest in the Lord.' Along the route to Hill Cliffe many blinds were drawn and many of the residents of the Stockton Heath dis- trict were present at the graveyard to pay a last tribute to one whose gotel works and sympathy were proverbial. EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

HEALTH OFFICERS AND POLITICS.

In the house of Commons on Tuesday, Visconnt Wolmer, (Newton, (C) asked the Chancellor of the Exchequer whether his attention has been called to a speech made by Mr J Roach of Leigh, Lancashire, on 03t0ber 15, at a meeting in support of a Labour candi- date; whether he was aware that Mr Roach had just been appointed an inspector under the National Insur- ance Act; and, if so, whether Government officials are entitled to make political speeches at party mettings. Mr Masterman replies: At the time when the speech referred to was made the gentleman in question was not an officer of the Commissioners, and was not therefore within their disciplinary control. All health officers understand that after appointment they must not make political speeches.

NEWTON DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting was held at the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Monday, Mr W Thompson presiding.— At a meeting of the General Parposes Committee prior to the Council it was decided not to proceed at present further with the application to the Board of Trade for , an Electric Lighting Order.—A letter was read from I Mr F F Burke, enclosing a petition from 31 Eanestown shopkeepers, requesting the Council to abolish the holding of stalls on the market on Saturday, as they considered it unfair to them, as rate- payers, that two market days should be allowed I each week.—lt was resolved that the subjeot be referred to the Market Inspector to report upon.— It was resolved with reference to the recent drown ing fatality in the canal that the Clerk be instructed to communicate with the L. and N.-W. Railway Co. as to the provision of means for protecting the public against such accidents, and that he also call the attention of the police to the need of adequate supervision. The minutes were approved on the proposition of Mr THOMPSON, seconded by Mr BORRON.

TECHNICAL SCHOOL.

On Saturday evening Sir Alfred Dale,Vice-Chancellor of the University of Liverpool distributed the prizes at the new Technical School at Earlestown, Mr C B F Borron presided. The Principal (Mr R ii Albery) in his annual report, deplored the lack of students in engineering subjects in such an industrial centre as Earlestown. The per- centage of successes in the examinations was 88, which was a very gratifying result. (Applause.) They had a fine building, an extensive laboratory, and a splendid set of class rooms, and he hoped the young apprentices in the district would show a livelier interest in the school. (Applause).

WARD CONTEST.

Sir,— Mr. Councillor Chritiles in his maiden spesch at the Conservative Club after the declaration of the poll,bemoans toe loss of my acquaintance and says he has been fighting something he could not see and that I ought to be made to pay the expense to which I have put the town. I am surprised at the audacity of his assumption in making such a statement. I received a bona 1 ide invita- tion from several burgesses in Howley Ward to allow myself to be nominated as a candidate end I conducted the election as I BM firmly convinced municipal elections should be fought--with out canvass, meetings or conveyances, and entirely free from political influence and bias. My election address in which my views were expressed was posted around the ward, and the decision was left severely to the electorate as to whether I was a fit and proper person to be entrusted with their confidence._ _ . .

If Mr Chrimes considers he has been put to ttuneces- sary expense, I would remind him it is of his own choosing and I assume him to be a free agent, con- trolling his own finances. Then let him "be a sport" and not übraid me for a matter upon which I was not c insulted. To the (,)S, electors who were good enough to record their votes in ray favour, I am deeply obliged, and though unsuccessful, I have hopes that the little leaven of temperance existing will ultimately prevail and purge not cnly Howley Ward but the whole town from the dominating influence so deplorably present at election periods.—Yours truly, F. J. OWEN.

TOWN HALL WARD ELECTION.

Sir,—t; ranting that you will allow me a small nortion of your valuable space I should like to make brief reference to the above. It seems to me that no one outside the realm of party politics has a Tight to organize for representation on the Town Council. What right have the Insurance men to run a candi- date? What has Insurance got to do with municipal affairs, and so on. These were acme of the silly qw.-s- -tions often asked last week. One might ask in return, what right has the Tory Club to run candidates, or what right have the Liberals to run candidates ? If they have a right have not we ? If it comes to a ques- tion of qualifications and all-round knowledge of town affairs, who should know better than an insurance man, who has been operating for say 10 or 20 years amongst the people of his own town, working constantly in almost every street, court, yard and alley ? And so we noble army of ' common club-men' ran a candidate. He was put in the field as an Independent, and so we had the political forces arrayed against us. The cry of some was: He won't get 120 vutes, but neverthe- less with outside interference thrown in, we polled the magnificent figures of 318. A good many people were disappointed that Mr Delaney did not run as a Liberal candidate, and because of the aforesaid fact they either voted for Mr Jenkins or did not vote at all. In other words : He is better as a Liberal than an Insurance Man. What rot to be sure! The position is almost as ridiculously amusing as Mr W P Rylands' tirade on the present Government when supporting a candidate in another ward. However in conclusion, I would remind those who were so bitterly opposed to the insurance men, that we as a body of men, are a force not to be treated lightly. We are about 300 strong in Warrington, and I doubt not that at some future time we shall have the audacity to put other candidates in the field, and with a clean, straight fight, and no backing out,' we shall I hope, live to win the day. Yours respectfu I:v, A " CLUB-MAN."

THE IRISH NATIONAL FORESTERS AND ST. JOHN'S WARD ELECTION. ..

Sir,—We desire to draw attention to a pester which was issued—unfortunately too late to b 3 answered before November Ist —in which Mr. T. S. Steel, the unsuccessful Conservative candidate, stated that the Irish National Foresters "should support him." Now other Irish societies—at least their prominent officials—are under the impression that Mr. Steel obtained permission to insert the name of the IN F. upon his election poster. This is not the case, and Mr. Steel had no authority or title to use it—nor indeed had he any right to expect our support. Mr. Steel would not be accepted as a member of the I.N.F. should he so desire, and as he professed to have such good knowledge of Friendly Societies, he should have known that the I.N.F. is " non-political and non-sectarian," having no desire or ambition to be involved in party politics—it is an independent party. How Mr Steel could have reasonably expected the I.N.F. to give support to his Farty, who have in the past done their utmost to thwart the holes and aspira- tions of the Irish people, we cannot say. Perhaps Mr Steel thought he could hoodwink our National organization as his party are " blustering " the Orangemen in Ulster. Had the I.N.F. taken any part in the St. John's Ward election Mr Steel would not have had its support; as to serving our interests in the Council we can trust them equatly to Mr Shaw— a member of a party which is doing its Ltxtost to elevate and prosper rreland and to whom we wish the best luck. However it would be ungrateful not to thank Mr steel for remembering us and we respect- fully thank him for his advertisement. "En passant" we might state that other Irish organisations in Warrington would do well to mind their own affairs—the Irish National Foresters are quite capable—like all Irishmen—of managing their own affairs without interference or commont from any other source. On behalf of the "Erin's Hope Branch" and the order in general we beg to thank you for the space occupied.—Faithfully yours. JAMES MORAN, S.C. Secretary. JOHN DOYLE, D Secretary.

THE MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS-SOME SUGGESTIONS.

Sir,—l have noticed a few notes of remarks made by the Rev W Bracecamp respecting the recent elections and also what the Mayor has had to say on the same subject. As one of the candidates at this election, I should like to say that I am in entire agreement with these gentlemen on this question and in spite of statments made to the contrary, endeavoured throughout to keep politics absolutely out of the question as far as I was personally concerned. I think you will agree that any candidate who sets out to do this honestly is heavily handicapped. In the first instance the probability is that apart from one or other of the political parties backing his nomination, he in the present condition of affairs stands very little chance of being brought for- ward at all. In the second place, if be desires to be absolutely unfettered, he must not be indebted to any party for financial support, and few men are able to continue to fight at their own charges. Once, or twice possibly. but afterwards the question is bound to arise, is it worth whiie ? I regret that there is, perhaps, little doubt of the fact that useful, good men, are kept out of our Council because of the political bitterness often introduced, and further no one is anxious for the notoriety (of a sort) which seems to be the necessary accompaniment of such a struggle. Is it not possible to effect a compromise? Do we really and honestly believe that in our Council a man is biassed for or against any proposal for the good of the town because of his political opinions? There are any amount of good, true, upright business men, surely in Warrington of all political complexions, who would be a credit to our Alunicipal life and if this is so and some of them are doubtless in our Council now why should they be put to the trouble, expense and worry of contested elections simply because they are or are not Liberal or Conservative in Imperial politics. Is it not possible, for instance, to have a Selection Committee, composed of all parties and representative of the town and its industries, who could approach men of standing and ability, apart from party altogether, and thus obtain for our Town Council the best it is possible to get to supervise its municipal affairs? Such a committee could watch attendances, votes and matters that come up generally and decide whether it was in the interests of the town that any person should be asked to serve again. If this could not be, could not some mutual arrange- ment be come to, to do away with all House-to-House Canvassing and restrict the Candidates to the issue of an Election Address and Public Meetings. We are all agreed that the canvass is unreliable and cannot be depended upon, and it is a fruitful source of bitter- ness, through false rumours and mis-statements made

THE MAYORALTY. COUNCILLOR DR. JOSEPH TO BE RE-ELEC fED TO-DAY.

At twelve o'clock noon to-day (Saturday), the newly- constituted Town Council will hold its first meeting. The most important business is the election of Mayor, and Councillor Dr. Joseph will be installed in that high office for the third year in succession. Then will follow the appointment of the Deputy-Mayor, Mayor's chaplain, the fixing of the quarterly and monthly meetings, the consideration of the Standing Orders, the election of presiding aldermen, the appointment of the Council committees, and of repre- sentatives on the following bodies :—Lancashire Inebriates Act Board, County Rate Joint Committee. Lancashire and Western Sea Fisheries Board, two , governors of Warrinizton Grammar School, members of the Council on the Local Pension Committee, members of the Education Committee; and co-optative members of the Education Committee. The Mayor will attend divine service at the Parish Church to-morrow' (Sunday).morning. Those taking part in the usual procession will assemble at the Town Hall at ten o'clock.

BUILDER SENTENCED FOR PE—RJURy. For perjury in an affidavit for the purpose of alma_ big his bankruptcy, Frederick George Pope, aged 49, a builder, was sentenced to 12 mutbs hard labour at the Old Bailey on Tuesday. For ,e defence it was stated that the perjury was committ.A during an excess of business worry.

November Bth, 1612.

VICAR AND THE MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS.

"There is a feeling abroad that if ycu put up a clothes prop, put some clothes on it and labelled it by some particular colour, there are people to be found who would vote for it," declated the Rev W Braceeamp in the course of a sermon on " The duties of citizen. ship" at St Paul's Church on Sunday evening. He deplored the fact that to-day the best men were stand- ing aloof from public work bscause of party politics (which had become more or less a curse to religion), which were being ridden to death. People were pett- ing sick and tired of the idea of politics. What they wanted was men of the right stamp, men whose char- acters were above suspicion and who would carry Out the wishes of the people. What was the use of vcting for people whe could not conduct their own affairs? And how, therefore, in the name of fortune could they expect them as the representatives of the people to look after the affairs of the town? They wanted men of business, men of position, men to whom they could look up, men to whom they could take off their hats in tha street because they were men of integrity and true members of the State Some people said they. took no part in party politics, and there were many who felt that was the right thing to do, but as a citizen of the State the conclusion was absolutely wrong. It was our duty to see that we carried out the talents committed to us honestly, squarely and rightly. The rev, gentleman also urged that young men should qualify themselves for the defence of their country by serving in the I erritorial forces.

WHIST DRIVE AT THE LIBERAL CLUB.

RE-OPENING OF THE BILLIARD ROOM.

The large billiard room at the Liberal Club has undergone extensive alterations end re-decoration and to celebrate the re-opening, a whist drive was held on Tuesday evening. There was an attendance of 140 and the function proved completely successful and enjoyable. The embellishment of the room, which has been most tastefully carried out by Messrs W Winstanley and Son, was greatly admired and the effect was heightened by a display of choice plants, arranged by the club manager (Mr WA Clay).Amongst those present were: Mr and Mrs A II Crosfield, Miss Crosfield, Miss Le Thicke, Alderman Tinuion, Councillor Wilkinson, Councillor and Mrs Peacock, Councillor and Mrs Lindley, Councillor and Mrs Halliwell, Mr and Mrs T Pemberton, Mr and Mrs T Locker, Mrs J T Loner, Mrs Walker, Mrs E V Walker, Mrs Osborne, Mrs J S Hill, Mr A Rathbone, Mr G Bowman, Mr W Webster, Mr W Bibby, and Mr J Critchley. The arrangements had been most efficiently carried out by Mr Herbert Johnson, as h ,n. secretary. Before the drive began, Councillor Wilkinson asked Mr Crosfield formally to open the billiard room. . . .

_ Mr Crosfield, who was greeted with enthusiasm, said it gave him great pleasure to open that magnificent room. He understood they had not yet finished their club alterations but that another important addition was to be made for them all, and not least for the ladies. (Applause) Having expressed admiration of the innovation of windows opening on to the bowling green, Mr Crosfield said they had always had a happy knack in their party of combining active politics with active recreation, and he thought that room would conduce to both purposss. He congratulated most sincerely all those who had taken part in carrying out that splendid improvement. (Applause). The whist drive was then entered upon, and after many interesting games, the winners were: - Ladies, 1, Mrs Owen; 2, Mrs Wood; consolationXiss Pitman. Gentlemen : 1, Mr J Davies; 2, Mr Simpson ; con- solation, Miss Short (playing as gentleman). The i catering was n the hands of Atkinson's Cafes Ltd. and gave every satisfaction.

AN INTERNATIOAL " WHITAKER."

" Whltaker's Alinanack " is known wherever the English language is spoken, and " You'll find it in Whitaker" has become a household word. The publishers are now preparing a new book, which will appeal with equal force to all who possess a knowledge of the English tongue, wherever they may happen to be residing. This new book, (" The International Whitaker") gives a graphic and succint account of every country in the world, and concerning every nation there is a mass of detail in a handy and convenient form, such as has never before been presented in a single volume. It is a " Whitaker of Every Nation." The book is to published early in December ; and its price, in cloth binding, is two shillings net.

WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY.

Concert in Parr Hall.

The opening concert of the Musical S iciety's season ‘Vedriesday evettL g proved very interesting and enj yable, 3et it at rst t. d. we regret to say, only a moderate attendance. The prngraintne, which was of a miscellaneous cnaracter, rontained c imparatively rp along, l.ttle to 4io for the sociaty's vocal members Charles Stanford's short chotal cantata Phaudrig CI (Moore." ell unaccompanied part song, and a chorus with orchestra comprising their entire contribution —the major part of the evening being t then up with soniz-i by Mde. Marie Brema, some violin solos by Mr. Nautu Blinder, and a few orchestral pieces. Toe story ot a national hero's stirring deeds, which forms the groundwork of Stautord's vividly picture-ue composition, would have been brought hi one to the audience more forcibly had the whole thing been made more emphatically and characteristically Irish in delivery, —a shortcoming which is the more regrettable because the work was, in other resp-cts, admirably presented. Regarding it as a choral test-piece, the collective singing oz the society's membets btought oat some excellent points,—the attack in numerous places for the vat Mos sections of the voices was always good, the toile, especially amongst the altos and the basses, was round and well-sustained, whim the alternations of vivaciausness and tender expressii n were most intelligently marked. Yet one felt that members had not fully grasped the composer's and the librettists intentiuns insofar as these required, as a sine qua non for a perfect presentment, that members should for the moment identify themselves in the closest possible way with the mannerisms of the people the incidents in whose lives they are setting themselves out to pourtray, and in Wednesdays' per- formance one longed to have more of the genuine "brogue ' even if one had to be content with less of the music. Nevertheless, with the full text of the story before them in the printed programme, the audience manifestly enteted into the spirit of the cantata and thoroughly enjoyed the musical telling of the escapades of the illustrious " Phaudrig," the enthu- siastic applause wnich was accorded it being seen to be a well-earned compliment to the society's combined fotces. For it must not be forgotten that Stanford's orchesiration in this work is far removed from the ordinary,and the admirable way in which the instrumentalists (supplemented as usual by professional help) backed up the chorus by well- played band parts, is therefore a matter for hearty congratulation. The singing (unaccompanied) of Coleridge Taylor's part-bong" The Lee Shore" gave a clear indication of some painstaking work done in rehearsal. It showed excellent precision and vigour in the opening unison passages, good intonation in the succeeding rugged harmonies underlying the sustained soprano notes, and notable purity of tone in certain passages for the alto voices. The later point at which the parts merge into more solid harmony was not entered upon with quite ideal tenderness, the united sopranos having apparently yet to learn how to come in upon a high note sotto yore, yet firmly. The conclusion of this fine part-song was marked by a distinctly massive and resonant effect, the pitch of the voices being well-sustained throughout its perform- ance. The same composer's rousing chorus with orchestra " The Vikings presented little in the way of difficulty and members thiew themselves into its rendering in a whole-hearted way which ensured success,

As might be expected, Mde. Marie Brema's singing bears the impress of ripe maturity and long experi- ence, and, while it is perhaps too much to expect that she should be able entirely to divest herself of the grand opera manner on the concert platform, there can be no rkubt that much that she sang on Wednesday evening suffered from an exaggerated method of interpretation. In " Che faro " from Gluck's "Orpheus" (sung for once, it is pleasing to note, in EnyziB7) Mde. Brema made a most favourable reappearance in Warrington after interval the length of which one would prefer not to compute, rendering its pathetic appeal in a manner which went to the hearts of all hearers and evoked the greatest enthusiasm. Later, one listened to four other songs by Mde Brema (including Schubert's " Erlking " and Humperdinck's " Wiegenlied ") and also to an encore song, and marvelled at a versatility which ran through every variety of emotion and impressed one in so many. Needless to say that Mde. Brema received quite an ovation, her recall time after time to the plat- form testifying to the audience's high appreciation of the efforts of the gifted and charming vocalist.

As a solo violinist Mr. Nautn Blinder is destined to take high rank. One felt it a real deprivation that, as the concert was concluded at 10, the whole of the Lichaikowsky concerto could not have been played by him. The first movement, with which alone the audience had to content themselves, served just to create an appetite for much more, and Mr Blinder's playing of it demonstrated, and his later solos confirmed the fact, that in his person we have the embodiment of a superb technique manifesting itself in temperamental qualities conspicuous amongst which are great breadth of style and unfathomed depths of feeling. For a most finished performance of the coupled solos "Le Cygne " (Saint SaenB), and " Mazourka " (Zaryzycki.) Mr. Blinder was vociferously encored. The overture to " Ruv Bias " was performed at th. opening of the concert with some approach to brilliance notwithstanding the obvious fact that players of stringed instruments were over-strained in coping with its executant difficulties. The " Valse lente ' from the pen of Mr. F. H. Crossley, the society's conductor, wag, on the other hand, most daintily presented, the pretty interplay amongst the various classes of instruments of its graceful subject- .matter being a striking feature of the composition, the performance of which was loudly applauded.
