CHIEF CONSTABLE SUMMONED. SEQUEL TO THE LEVERSON CASE. Goods Returned to Purchasers.

The Chief ConstaLle of Warrington (111—r-. Martin Nicholls) figured in the new r6le of defendant at the Borough Police Court on Thursday, when he was summoued for the return of jewellery and other articles held by the police in connection with the recent charges of larceny against Julia Leverson, who was acquitted at the Liverpool Quarter Sessions. Mr. T. S. Steel, solicitor, made the application on behalf of Henry and Eliza Maddock, William and Sarah Ellen Bruce and Annie Throssell, who had purchased the goods from Mrs. Leverson. The magistrates on the bench were Messrs J Rymer Young (in the chair), A J Jolley, W T Williams, J Upson, W Roberts and Dr Naden.

!slrC E Burgis, barrister (instructed by Mr T S Steel) appeared for the applicants. He said that the goods Mrs Maddock claimed were a silver tray, electro plated kettle and stand, a gold watch and a quantity of Irish linen. Mrs Bruce claimed the return of a diamond bracelet, a diamond ring and a dressing case. Mrs brosselPs claim was for a gold watch and a bundle of bed linen. The application was brought under the Act to make further provision in respect of the disposal of property in the possession of the police, and he asked the Bench to make an order for the delivery of the property to the persons whom he represented. The property came into his clients' possession in May and June when they purchased it from Julia Leverson. The goods had been bought by her from tradespeople in the town. After the property had been bought by the applicants, proceedings were taken by the police against Mrs Leverson in that Court and they were asked to hand over to the police some of the goods in order that the case might proceed. The result of the prosecution was the acquittal of Mrs Leverson. So far as any charge of larceny was con- cerned, there was no foundation tor it, because the local magistrates had stopped one ca.e and in the other Mrs Leverson was acquitted. The police had no right to retain the goods wnich bad been handed to them to enable them to proceed against Mrs Leverson, and therefore the claimants who had purchased the goods from Mrs Leverson must be the persons entitled to them. Proceedings bad been taken by Messrs W Hodgkieson Ltd., and Messrs Miller and Co for the recovery of certain goods but both cases had now been abandoned, showing that those firms had no title to the artich s. As the police and the sellers of the goods had no claim, his clients as the purchasers were the only persons who should have the property. Mr Browne said he appeared for Messrs Miller and Co. A great deal that his learned friend bad said was true in certain respects but be had rot told the Bench the ridiculous prices the claimants paid for the goods. The value of the goods in the possession of the Chief Constable and belonging to Messrs Miller and Co, amounted to ,ClB4. They did not know that those pro- ceedings were being taken until the previous day and as tar as he could see, there was ouly one object in bringing forward the case that day and that was the County Court. Mr nurgis : What object, what does he mean by the County Court ?

Mr Browne : Because you thought I should be stuck in the County Court. Mr Burgis : We could not have had that anticipa- tion iu our mind, because wa had no idea Mr Browne would be here at all to-day. The party concerned is the Chia! Constable.

I.slr Browne : Imagine Nlssrs. Miller and Co., being swindled out of .ClB4 without being here to-day. suggest and I think the Cnief Coustahle will have no objection to the case lating adjourced until a week to- day to enable us to take out a summcns against the Chief Constable to show cause v‘hy he should not hand tack to us, the rightful owners beyond doubt, all this valuable property. It would be most iniquitous to make an order to-day without our having been heard. Mr Burgis said Mr Brownie bad had a long time in which to issue a summons but be had not done so. Therefore he could not complain about the claimants taking proceedings. Mr Browne had thrashed the question out in the County Court day after day and he uad lost the very issue they were trying tuere that day. Mr Browne : No! No!

Mr Burgis: He has lust inasmuch as he has abandoned his claim. If a man had a substantial and good claim, he would not abandon it. Surely it does not lie in his power to come here and say "These are my goods." Mr Browne: Why du my honourable friends not come to my terms ? Mr Burgis : We have offered to return them to you before the case commenced at the price given by my clients for them.

Mr lirov•ne : We should have held out both bands for them.

Mr Burg's said his clients bad taken up an absolutely honourable attitude. They had offered to restore the goods to the vendors on condition that they purchased them back at the price his clients paid for them and also that the vendors pay the applicants' casts. That wits before the County Court proceedings and Judge Stanger said it was au honourable proposal. Mr Browne ref used to accept. and went on to assert his legal rights Mr tirowne : This is not true.

Mr Burg's ; He tried to assert his legal rights and failed. in Going that he had put his (Mr Burgis) clients to an enormous cost. Mr Browne, having tailed to assert his legal rights,now came and said he wanted to purchase the gooos back. Mr Burgiss then read correspondence between his clients and Mr Browne showing that Mr Browne bad refused their terms, which were that the vendors should pay the applicants the amount they pur- chased the goods for and also pay the taxed coats. Judge Stanger had said it was a reasonable offer made in an honourable way and had be been in the case he would not have been prepared to deal with the matter on any other basis. Mr B. owne having failed r efore the Judge, time to that court with the hopi of getting judgment. Mr Browne : 'Nothing of the kind. Will the Bench consider my application for an adjournment? Mr Browne further stated that they bad paid the taxed costs in the County Court and withdrawn any imputa- tiams against Mrs Maddock. They had offered to repay the amount paiu for the articles by Mrs Maadock. Mr Burgis : He did not do that until he had put us to an enormous expense in proceeding with our claim. Mr Roberts (magistrate) said that when the last case against Mrs Leverson was tried at that court, the magistrates male the sugg-stiou al the close of the proceedings that some amicable arrangement should be come to by the parties for the disposal of the property. Mr Burgis : When my friend thought he had a legal remedy he was prepared to assert it, but when he fails he comes here for sympathy. The Chairman said they could not conside: the application for an adjournment. Mrs Maddock gave evidence in support of the application. She was asked to produce the goods in order that the police proceedings could be taken on the understanding that she was to have them back at the close of the case. -

Cross-examined by the Chief Constable witness said she received the goods from Mrs Leverson but did not get a receipt. The Chief : Is it customary when you go to a shop to pay such a sum as you paid and not get any acknowledgement of payment ?—I bought the goods and I was told they were coming out of pawn. It is usual to get a receipt in a shop but in this particular case I did not, as it was not a shop transaction.

In reply to the Chief Constable, Mrs Maddock said she did notimake any inquiries with regard to the bona-fides of Mrs Leverson.

Mr Burgis : Mrs Leverson was carrying on business in Museum-street in a big way, with six girls working for her.

Mr Bruce spoke to purchasing jewellery from Mrs Leverson. He said that if he had had the slightest idea that anything was wrong, he would snot have had any- thing to do with the woman. Toe Chief Constable: All along you have been pre- pared to return these goods if the money you paid for them was returned to you ?—Up to a certain time. We changed our minds when proceedings were taken against Mrs Maddock. The Chief Constable told the Bench that both the tradesmen and the purchasers bad been duped by Mrs Leverson, although the purchasers ought to have known that the stories she told them could not possibly have been true. No doubt they thought she had come by the goods honestly, but they had not taken sufficient care. Mrs Leverson, who had no means whatever, and was a swindler, was defended on her trial, and by strategy she got off. • Before the trial, the police, with every desire to see Justice done, interviewed one of the witnesses in regard to taking proceedings against Mrs Leverson for obtain- ing money by false pretences. When they interviewed Mrs Bruce she went away from her statement, so that they were unable to proceed with the charge of obtain- ing money by false pretences. When the case had been dismissed by the magistrates, the Chairman said he hoped some amicable arrangement would be come to in regard to the disposal of the property. The case bad, however, lingered on at an enormous expense to both sides, and it was a highly unsatisfactory business throughout.. The parties could easily have been brought together, and it was most regrettable that that application was made that day. The magiatrates retired, and on their return the Chairman said they had decided that the goods should be given np to the claimants. They expressed great regret that some amicable arrangement had not been arrived at, and that it had been found necessary to come there at all.

COUNTY COURT ACTION WITHDRAWN. Later in the day Mr Burgin appeared before his Honour Judge Stanger FC.C. at the County Court and mentioned the case of Hodgkinson v Maddock. He said it would be remembered that the action was adjourned at the last sitting of the Court. Since then criminal proceedings had been taken against Mrs Leverson for larceny. She was acquitted and his clients had now bad notice of discontinuance of the County Court Action. He asked his Honour to certify for advice on evidence. Mr Browne submitted that there was no necessity for advice on evidence.

His Honour, however, issued the certificate asked for and the action 1a accordingly abandoned.

Longdin, Alderman and Mrs Smethurst,Mr Peter Leigh, "Fred and Ella," Mrs Randall, Mr Thornton," Ann and Willie " (Reddish), Mrs Henshall and Gertrude, Alderman Tinnion and family, Mr Albert Taylor, Mr and Mrs Williamson and family. Mr aud Mrs William Roberts and family, Mr Harold Dutton, Mrs Walter H Young and family, Mr and Mrs Herbert Gibson, Miss Ida Haddock, Mr and Mrs Jacob Sankey and family, Mrs and Miss Fairclough, " Lizzie," Miss Holehonse, Mr Edwin Bradshaw and family, Mr and Mrs Coleman, Mrs Ewart and family, Mrs C Roberts and family, Mr and Mrs John Mainwaring and family, Mr and Mrs J Richardson, Mr and Mrs Pitchford, Mr and Mrs W Winstanley, Mrs Briggs (Wilderspool), Mr and Mrs Rudge, Mr and Mrs A W Howard, Mr and Mrs Evans, Mr and Mrs C Hughes. The funeral arrangements were in the hands of Messrs Robert Garnett and Sons, Warrington and Liverpool. References to the self-sacriticing work of Mrs Forshaw in connection with the Church were made at Holy Trinity Church and Christ Church, Latchford, on' Sunday morning. The family attended the service at Holy Trinity Church. Chopin's Funeral March was played on the organ at Christ Church, Latchford.
